PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

EXTRAORDINARY AERIAL PHENOMENON, flight of steps." UNLIKE the majority of things in this queer world, fogs are always mist till they are gone. A LITTLE GIRL describes a snake as "a thing that's a tail all the way up to its head." • THE "GRATE UNKNOWN."—One that will really "save half your coals." A R-:OT and shoe shop hangs out the sign, "Cast-iron Lasts!' We all know it does, but we don't want any boots made of it, just the same. GAOLER in German lock-up (to prisoners) : "If you don't stop that singing and whistling, I'll throw you both into the street !" A SOUND DEFENCE.—The Old Man: " Humph ! When I was your age I didn't wear kid gloves and carry a cane!" Algy (in an injured tone): "Well, father, I should think you'd expect to find some improvement in the family since that time." IT is popularly supposed that a man is most courageous when his stomach is full. But the nightmare that nearly scares the life out of us usually comes after a hearty supper. A POETIC Hibernian explains that love is com- monly spoken of as a "flame" because its a "tinder sentiinent." MIGHT BE WORSE. —Mrs. Youngbride : "George seems to be different, somehow, since our mar- riage." Mrs. Sinnick " Tline will remedy. that, my dear. He'll soon be indifferent. INQUIRING INFANT: "Ma, what is a nepotist ?" Ma : " A person who favours his relations." In- quiring Infant: "Then I suppose I'm one, for pa always says I favour you." "1 WANT to see the head of the house," said a pedlar to Mrs. Darley, who had answered the ring. "I am sorry," she replied, as she gently closed the door, "but baby is asleep just now." IN THE HEART OF LONDON. —Gentleman (to Irish valet) : " You're late again, Mike ! I told you, when I engaged you, that you'd have to be up with the lark." Irish Valet: "Show me the lark, sorr, an' Oi'll be up wid it ! " "Now, remember Belinda," said Mrs. Sharply to her daughter just married, "violence in anydiing is most unladylike. Of course, you will some- times disagree with your husband, but always hit him with the soft end of the broom."

THE following epigrammatic quatrain was written by a well-known bachelor-poet in a lady's album: "All honour to woman—the sweetheart, the wife, the delight of the fireside by night and by day, who never does anything wrong in her life, except when permitted to have her own way." RAILWAYS are like laundresses—they have ironed the whole country, and occasionally do a little .mangling. THOUGH a woman can keep a secret if she has to, she can't keep secret the fact that she has a secret to keep. IT is said that history repeats itself, and yet, even with this assistance, it is not every scnool boy who can repeat his history. IRISH MAGISTRATE: "Were you prisint whin the assault was committed on ye?" Witness: "May it plase the court, I had just got there." A MUSICAL critic, in descanting upon the superior musical taste of his town, says :—" Our ears have been cultivated till they overshadow all our other organs." CIRCUMSTANCES ALTER CASES. Unmarried Lady: "It must be a great thing when husband and wife are of one mind." Married Lady: "That depends on whose mind it is." PAT'S EXACTNESS. —Mike : "An' what are ye diggin' out that hole for, Pat ?" Pat: " Arrah an' it's not the hole I'm diggin'. out. I'm diggin' tbe dirt and lavin' the hole !" GENTLEMAN: "I see, waiter, that attendance is charged for in the bill, so I shall not give you a tip. [presume you don't expect one?" Waiter (sarcastically) : "Oh, no; I only expect tips from gentlemen ! ' FASCINATING WIDOW: "And what are you doing nowadays?" Gentleman: "Oh, amusing myself looking out for Number One. And you?" She: "Looking out for Number Two." IT is only guileless boyhood that vows he "will never do it again." Even when caught in the act, the full-grown man of sound mind tries to prove that he didn't do it at all. " TO3IMIE," said the teacher, "define the word widow—what is a widow ? " "A widow," began Tommie, slowly, "is a—a woman —" " Yes?" said the teacher. "A woman what wants to get married again," continued Tommie. "My dear sir," said the doctor, "it you will go on drinking like that you'll get--' "Well, what?" snapped the old man. "The technical name for it is delirium tremens." " Wed, that won't hurt me. I'm a professional snake. charmer." "AND you were very successful as a mission- ary ?" "Yes." "Did you get the heathen to believe that the whale swallowed Jonah ?" "Yes. They didn't think it remarkable at all, after I told them how many people had swallowed the story." NOT GOING Up.—One evening a vessel going down the Thames was brought to a atop by a thick fog. An impatient passenger went forward to the pilot, and the following dialogue took place Passenger: "Why can't we go on ?" Pilot: "'Cause we can't see the water, sir." Passenger: "But you can see the stars!" Pilot: "Yes, but we ain't goin' up there unless the b'iler busts." "AN old negro came before the bench, charged with some trivial offence. "Haven't you a lawyer, old man?" inquired the judge. "No, sah." "Can't you get one." "No, sah." "Don't you wan't me to appoint one to defend you?" "No, sah ; I jes' tho't I'd leab the cast to de ign'ance of de co't." A LAWYER'S PUN.—Mr. Justice Page was re- nowNed for his ferocity upon the bench. While upon circuit, a facetious lawyer named Crowe was asked if the judge was not just behind. "I don't know," said Crowe; "but if he is, I am sure he was never just before." ONCE a careless man went to the cellar and stuck the candle in what he thought was a keg of black sand. He sat near it, drinking wine, until the candle burned low. Nearer and nearer it got to the black sand—nearer and nearer, until the blaze reached the black sand—and, as it was nothing but black sand, nothing happened. LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAREST Nzuan,

Pale pinks and browns are judiciously blended together in millinery, thus affording a quiet con- trast to some daring combinations of blue, violet, and cerise. Little flat caps of sealskin and astrachan vie in favour with the new high-crowned felts, which bear a strong resemblance to the hats that were worn by the witches of fairyland lore. With these latter, wear the double-width veil, as it is no longer considered necessary to tie a narrow strip of net over the fringe when a wide-brimmed hat is affected. A pretty winter bat has a grey felt brim, edged with a narrow border of mink fur. The crown is puged in the style of a Tam-W- Shanter, and is fashionod in a dark shade of grey- blue velvet ; while the plumes of ostrich feathers are in grey, shading to brown, and fall gracef ally over the hair at the back. A charming model is a large hat made of black velvet with a satin crown. Two bows, a lavish amount of feathers and para. dice plume are its decorations.

A charming frock of velveteen for a little girl is cut square back and front, made over a camisole of of white silk, with sleeves trimmed with a puff, headed with four frills of silk, bordered with an infinitesimal lace. Such a pretty frock this is, and suited to a child of almost any age, from four to fourteen. A most effective pelisse shows a combina- tion of pale blue-face cloth and chinchilla. A costume for a little boy of five boasts smart little knickerbockers of blue serge, an open coat to match, which displays a waistcoat and collar of an indescribably patterned red and white cloth, spotted with white. These waistcoats, made in one piece with the collar, are a happy thought, for they supply just the note of colour needed in little boys' suits; they save the trouble of the linen collar, while they are much softer to wear, and more comfortable for the children altogether. A little girl's smart mantle is in ivory Bengaline, With collar and cape edged with white Thibet lamb fur. The hat is of Bengaline, the full brim having a fancy edge, with a Tam o' Shanter crown and cream plumes.

The wants of women in the matter of morning gowns are many. One is no longer allowed to "play many parts ;" it fills its own role and nothing more. If you cycle you want a special gown, or if you shoot, or p%y a country visit, or live in town, the costumes suitable for each occasion are distinct. An outdoor dress which has just been introduced is made of fawn cloth, the skirt plain, the bodice describing an open coat with a short ba.sque and large square revere, trimmed with guipure and narrow bands of velvet. The front is filled in with blue silk in the form of a yoke and pouch, trimmed also with guipure. It has bishop's sleeves, which are surely making their way. One of the new figured poplins which require to be better known, as they have much to commend them, formed another dress. This was shot in green and the new magenta red, the foot frill headed by bead trimming, one half of the bodice of magenta velvet, with the poplin crossing from the other side. A bow of green shot ribbon fell down the skirt, and a great deal of style was given by trimmings of brown seal and a velvet collar. A pretty costume is that known as the Addison made in terra-cotta Venetian cloth, with short open Eton jacket and douele-breasted Tatter- sall vest.

Anyone who is very energetic in the way of needlework can employ herself in working a dress in a new w%y. A friend is trying the experiment, and 80 far it is eminently successiul. The work is simply cloth applique on cloth, and the effect is that of a heavy brocade. O course, you can applique velvet on satin, or vice versa, but that is more expinsive and no,, so potty. This girl has set to work on a tan c loured cloth, which she is going e applique on a darker brow% The pattern is a bold conventional one, n 1 she is going to do just eneugh for a bodice, halt sleevas, and a broad band round the foot of the skirt. lier bodice is to be quite plain and full to the waist, where it will be ;,stoned in with a broad belt. The sleeves are to he made tigar, to the elbow of the applique stuff, and then they are to have hue puffs of the light material. A tea gown of stuff appqqued on velvet would look well, the applique portion to form the train and back. .

Yocre JANNIS.

THE EXAMINER, SATUP.DAY DECEMBER 1, 1894.

About one thousand servants are attached to the oyal household of Great Britain. The management of the Austrian female prisons is in the hands of female religious orders. Mrs. Deborah Brown, a coloured woman of Toronto, is said to have been born in Maryland, August 10, 1776. A daughter aged eighty-four lives with this mother of one hundred and eighteen. Mrs. Charles Robinson, of Lawrence, Kas., wife of the Governor, told the story of his life the other day to a phonograph cylinder, which is to be preserved by the State Historical Society. When a Chinese girl is married, her attendants are always the oldest and ugliest women to be found in the neighbourhood, who are paid to act as foils to her beauty. It is said that some exceptionally ugly old women snake their living by acting as professional attendants at weddings. . In the annals of isialingering,deafness is said to be the hardest to feign. One doctc r testifies: "I have scarcely , known a pretended deaf man who could not be found out by the simple expedient of dropping a coin behind him. It is so natural to turn round when there is money to be picked up. Miss Margaret Young, an intelligent and educated young woman, has recently been appointed Queen of the Mantua group of the South Pacific Islands, by the natives, against her will. The people regard her as a goddess, and do not allow her outside her cabin limits unless carried on a litter by her maids, and when she ventures forth, her subjects are ordered, under pain of death, to keep hidden in their homes. Mrs. Kendal is accredited with a truly wonderful method of housekeeping. In every room of the house hangs a little slate, with pencil attached. On her daily tours she records on these slates criticisms and comments developed by the condition of each respective room, and the maid, following her, acts accordingly. If the latter has any legitimate reply to make she puts it at the end of the mistress's note, and by this means all jarring is saved and nothing is forgotten, at least so she says. A young Japanese lady, Miss Shids Mori, is at present studying for the missionary work in her own country at Radcliffe College, United States. She has recently been giving her opinion on the way the American ladies dress. She says : " I do not think your dress is so pretty as the Japanese dress, and the waists of your dresses I \do not like. We do not wear any corsets, you know, with our Japanese dress, and we are so much more com- fortable all the time, especially In the summer. But the lower part of your dresses seems better to me ; the underwear and the skirts I like, they are easier to get I about in."

But the t

Miss Emily Faithfull was a pioneer of the fullPr opportunities and th. greater liberty enjoyed by women to-day. All her thoughts and efforts have been bestowed on the advancement of her set. She has laboured strenuously in promoting the industrial and educational interests of women. More than thirty years ago Miss Faithfull took up her work, and was almost alone in the field. She founded a women's printing office, with women compositors, and through it issued the first number of the "Victoria Magazine," of which she was editress for eightren rears. Miss Faithfull has lately re- ceived a grant from the Royal Bounty Fund.

Flow to Clean Black Lace Veils.

Take some new milk, add a little water to it, lay the veil flat, take a niece of net or old veil, rub both' sides, shake them well, have your irons hot, and lay fine muslin over and iron. I have done some this summer that have lain aside for years, and they are now like new. Some were all spotted with timid. Don't lay paper to iron on, it sticks to the net.

Crnss Stitch.

The cross stitch of the Germans is identical with the English cross stitch, the only difference bin in the material on which it is worked. English cress stitch is RenPrally worked on an open canvas ; the German on thick material. It presents no difficulty to the worker beyond the countinr, of the stitches upon a pattern. The materials hest suited to it for washing purposes are the German canvases (which are woven so that each thread is clear and distinct), Java awl honeycomh can- vas, coarse linens, and linens divided into squares When heavy materials are used as hackgrounds,in which the threads cannot be counted, the work is done ove woolwork canvas, the threads of which are drawn away when completed.

What Prostrates Women. It Is almost impossible to name an age at which every woman is at her hest. writes Reheeca Harding Davis in the "Lades' Florne Journal." If you examine, indeed, into the effect of a forced mental growth upon her body, you may write tomes. A witty French woma.n,who was here last winter, saw one side of that subject. 66 Mi. no !" she sighed. "We women in Paris do net granule with such grave studies as von in America W- not co-operate ;we have no public virtues. But." with a shrug, "neither have we nerve prostration !" Thermic. cerirral assertion which one can safely make is that every woman is at her best in body and mind at the ace when she is most fully ocr,upied with her true work in the world, whether that he art, cookery, or lecturing, provided that she goes to it simply and humbly. It is not their work that prostrates the nerves of wnm.n rvp vulgarises their natures. It is the incessant squabbling and posing and boasting about their work.

THE DOUBLE LIFE: OR, THE HAMPTON MYSTERY. By the Author of " DARKWOOD."

CHAPTER XLIII. IN THE LION'S DEN.

I am one, my liege, Whom the vile blows and buffets of the world Have so incensed, that I am reckless what I do. MACBETH. The Lady Beatrice, under the ministrations of her alarmed father and Lord Trevelyan, was speedily aroused from her swoon. She opened her eyes with a frightened start, and sprang half upright, looking around her with glances of wild &ism. "Be calm' Beatrice !" said the earl, soothingly, taking her hand. "The marquis and I under- stand how this discovery of Geoffrey's existence agitates you I My poor child I And you have loved him all these years ?" Lord Trevelyan looked down upon her ladyship with a stamp expression of tenderness. " Beatrice " he saki, softly, "I have thought you cold and heartless. I believed that your soul was in your social triumphs. How I have mis- understood you I Forgive me, Beatrice. Let us be friends 1" He held out his right hand, large, white, and shapely. The Lady Beatrice made a gesture to take it, and then drew back, shuddering. "You shrink from me, because I am his uncle," said Lord Trevelyan, sorrowfully. "I cannot blame you, Beatrice. You have suffered at his hands. But I will avenge your wrongs," he added, with stern emphasis. "Geoffrey Trevelyan shall yet weep more bitterly than you have wept—unless —unless—" The Lady Beatrice dropped her head yet lower. "Unless what? "she asked, faintly. "Unless he will give me back Giralda," and the sternness melted from the face of the old marquis suddenly, as ice melts in the sun. "I love that girl, Beatrice. She is as innocent and pure and tender ne an angel. She loved me too! I frightened her, I terrified her, I showered abuse on her! She can never forgive me. Her love for me must have turned to loathing, but I must see her again! I must have her back !" He turned away and walked to the fire, to hide the convulsive workings of his countenance. The earl felt his daughter's hand flutter in his own, but he could not see her face. "Poor Beatrice 1 " he whispered, in fatherly pity and sympathy. "And all these years I could see no deeper than the world! I could not guess that you carried a hopeless love and sorrow in your heart! Forgive me—" "Don't father I " inturrepted the Lady Beatrice, in a sharp whisper. "I cannot bear it.' She rose as she spoke, declining his assistance by a gesture and went from the room, ascending to her own apartments. The earl and the marquis stood by the fire dis- cussing her and the strange news Lord Trevelyan had brought. "You have decided, then, Trevelyan," said Lord Hampton, presently, "that you will forgive Geof- frey and take him back, on condition that Giralda returns to you?" The fiery old marquis shook his head. "You mistake me," he said, with harsh intona- tion. "1 shall never forgive Geoffrey. I shall never take him back. But I will forbear to pros- ecute him—l will simply ignore his existence—if Giralda will return to me in love and forgive

ness 1" "And when you are gone, Geoffrey will step in- to your place as if nothing had aver happened?" The voice of the marquis trembled as he an. : "This is a sore subject, Hampton. My mind is In a sort of chaos between my love and my hatred. The bitterness and vengefulness of years has been Bet against an affectisn of to-day. 1 don't quite know my own mind. At times I would give all to revenge myself on that viper, Geoffrey, and then I yearn for the sight of that blue-eyed girl, with her dark hair, and pure, sweetface, and'l could give up hatred and revenge to feel her soft arms around my neck, her cheek pressed to mine, or her sunny head in my bosom. swered. "Beatrice, I have told him all I" " All ?" "Yes, Beatrice, true hearted wife ! he knows all—who you are—who 1 am—all this mystery I I should have preferred that he should hear the story from his mother's lips. You could have told it more delicately than I. I told him where Giralda is, of the position in life that will yet be his, and of your danger. Beatrice, it was well done. I did not dream the boy was so noble. He seems to have sprung at once from childhood into a tender, gentle, thoughtful manhood. He has taken my burdens on himself. He seems to have acquired a reverence for me; and you he worships for your brave, noble, and unfaltering trust, through sorrow and shame and years of darkness." There were glad tears in the eyes of the Lady Beatrice. She had dreaded the effect of the story on her spirited young son. "I cam" to you this morning to tell you all this," resumed Geoffrey, "and to inform you of my new address. I was also impatient to learn if you have any news of Fay. Has Adlowe made known to you our poor boy's prison. Has Lord Grosvenor dis- covered him ?" "Fay is safe. He is with Giralda." Geoffrey Trevelyan looked at once joyful and puzzled. "I had a letter from Giralda this morning," continued the Lady Beatrice, producing it. "Read it." The father read it with gathering amazement. His existence made known to Lord Trevelyan, Giralda expelled from the Park and become a wanderer, her meeting with Fay and her present residence with her young brother at the little town of Dalton—all these were facts which greatly ex- cited him. "What is to be done, Beatrice ? " he asked restor- ing to her the letter. " Meggy Fleck is still at The Evergreens in charge," responded her ladyship- "I received this letter in my father's presence. A few minutes since I went upstairs and despatched Mary Fleck to The Evergreens with a message to Meggy to go on to Dalton at once. It almost crazes me to think of those two children there unguarded, and Lord Adlowe searching for Giralda. Dalton is but little more than twenty miles distant from Hampton- wold. I shall make some excuse to my father and set out for Hamptonwold to-Morrow morning. I am all anxiety to be near my children." "The cord seems to be tightening its folds around us, Beatrice," said her husband, sadly. "The Evergreens is no longer a shelter for our loved ones. Where shall we gather our family together again ? " "We will find a new home after a little, dear," said the Lady Beatrice, gently. "For the present you must be in hiding. How would you like to take Rupert and go over to France ?" "And leave you and Giralda alone to bear Ad- lowe's persecutions? No; but as you are going clown to Hamptonwold, I could take Rupert with me and visit Lord Grosvenor. I think we would be safe there." The Lady Beatrice approved the suggestion. "In that case," added Trevelyan, " what shall be done with Giralda and Fay ?' "I do not know. I will think the matter over, and devise some refuge for them. At this moment soy brain is not clear, I am full of fears, Geoffrey. Who do you think is in the drawing-room ?" "Your father, the earl, perhaps.' "Oh, my husband, you do not, cannot suspect into what dangers you have blindly rushed. My father is there. So is Detective Rush, the man who traced me to The Evergreens. And so is Lord Trevelyan, full of a vindictive hatred against you." Geoffrey Trevelyan's face paled under its coating of skilfully applied paint and powder. "My uncle here?" he cried, in agitation. The Lady Beatrice, in reply, told him all the in- cidents connected with the visit of the marquis. The question of permitting Giralda to return to his protection was folly considered, and both parents decided to leave its solution with the young girl herself. "Stern and haughty and cynical as he is," said the Lady Beaaice, "Giralda really loves him." There was a sorrowful quivering light in Trevel- yan's eyes, and he said, sighing : 'I can understand that, Beatrice. He is, under all his faul!s, a grand old man. I love him yet. He has been, to use a trite old phrase, more sinned against than sinning.' " There ensued a brief silence. The Lady Beatrice, in her character of loving wife, nestled in her husband's broad bosom, a happy contentment en- foia mg her storm-tossed soul, a delicious peace and hopefulness expressed in her looks and manner. Trevelyan felt the charm, and his anxieties and uneasiness gave way to quiet resolution. An hour passed there in quiet converse among the nestling shadows, and the fire glowed dully in the grate, and the lance of April sunshine shone on the carpet, and the two lovers—for they were true and tender lovers, all the truer and tenderer for their years of secret .wedlock—communed with each other, and gathered strength from their very so, rows. t length the Lady Beatrice started, as the hall clock struck. " You must go, my husband," she said, with a sigh. "It is not safe for you to remain here so long, with the marquis and that detective in the house. How strange it seems to sit here with you in this old music-room, wliere we sat together and made love so many years ago. I never thought to bring the better part of my double life within these walls. You will start for the Eagle's Eyrie, will you not, this very night? I dread you remain- ing in London." Trevelyan gave the desired promise. "And be sure to cover your tracks. This Mr. Rush, the detective, saw you passing the drawing. room door. He has taken a fee from me, but Lord Trevelyan may have outbid me. He may try to follow you to your lodgings, in order to be- tray you, or make money out of the knowledge. You must be on your guard, Geoffrey; and, oh, I wish you would adopt another disguise when you leave town. lam very anxious." Trevelyan declared that he would do as she wished, and then came the bitterness of parting. It was soon over. Then the "professor," pale, quiet, and subdued, with bowed figure slowly emerged from the music- room into the wide hall. At the same moment, apparently by the merest 'coincidence, the door of the drawing-room opened; and Detective Rush, bowing and smiling, appeared on the threshold, attended by Lord Hampton and Marquis Trevelyan. The Lady Beatrice comprehended the situation at a glance, as she followed her visitor. In an instant she was her haughty society-self, yet V.easantly condescending and-patronising. "You may rely upon me, professor," she said, in a low tone, which still the quick hearing of the detective caught. "I will use my influence in your behalf. You may refer any one to me." The " professor " murmured his thanks. The group at the door of the drawing-room lin- gered until the visitor of the Lady Beatrice came up. Muttering an apology, Rush then stepped aside in such a manner as to leave the " professor " and Lord Trevelyan face to face. It was a trying moment for two of that group. The marquis looked frankly and unsuspiciously into the face of the supposed music-master. Some- thing in the apparently aged taco seemed to hold his gaze. And Geoffrey's blue eyes, behind their quaint spectacles, grew blurred with sudden tears. The sight of that grand old face, with its keen, black eyes and rugged features, stirred his inmost soul. His lordship was the only father he had ever known, and he had loved him—he still loved him. This was the first meeting between the two since the hour that had wrecked the faith of the elder man in the younger. Geoffrey felt faint and ill. An iron hand seemed grasping at his heart. A thousand boyish memories swept over his soul. He gave a gasping sigh, bowed, and passed on leaning heavily on his staff, a strange sense of weakness half prostrating him. A moment later he had made his egress from the house. Another moment, and Detective Rush was also in the street, vacant of aspect, abstracted in manner, yet closely and keenly watching and following the disguised fugitive. (To be continued).

" CAPITAL " CHAT.

Already an agitation has begun for the abolition of the cumulative system of voting. The school board election has so completely established the principle of letting the minority govern and of de- priving an overwhelming majority of every shred of power, that the Progressives will immediately add a reform of the school board system of votinz to their programme. Its assimilation to the county council method of election will have the effect, at least, of making the board truly representative of the majority of the voting electorate,and of freeing it from the anomaly which the cumulative vote has created of giving the honours of war and the control of education for the next three years to the numerically inferior party.

Mr. Gladstone's taste in sermons has not been much of a secret, but now we have it put on re- cord by himself. The " Prestyterian" newspaper publishes an expression of opinion from Mr. Glad- stone as to the sermons he likes best. It is in the following terms :—" One thing I have against the clergy, both of the country and in the town. I think they are not severe enough on their congre- gations. They do not sufficiently lay upon the souls and consciences of their hearers their moral obligations and probe their hearts and bring up their whole lives and action to the bar of con- science. The class of sermons which I think are most needed are of the class which offended Lord Melbourne long ago. Lord Melborne was seen one day coming from a church in the country in a mighty fume. Finding a friend, he exclaimed: 'lt is too bad! I have always been a supporter of the church, and I have always upheld the clergy. But it is really too bad to have to listen to a sermon like that we have had this morning. Why, the preacher actually insisted upon applying religion to a man's private life!' But that is the kind of preaching which I like best—the kind of preaching which men need most; but it is also the kind of which they get the least."

The feature of this year's Stanley Cycle Show, now going on at the Agricultural Hall, is the Bam- boo bic3 cle or tricycle,which is not only very hand- some but very light, and, according to reports very strong. Twenty-five of them are exhibited by a firm, and there is nothing in the show which arrests so much attention. That they will be also very much cheaper than the machines now in use need 'hardly be said ; but the cheapness should add immensely to the popularity of cycling in general and of the bamboo make in particular. The power of doing good work is claimed for it, as one machine is shown on which more than a thousand miles have been ridden by a man of lei stone. The one misgiving of the professional and amateur racer is whether the strength of the machine can be always depended upon for good racing work under all circumstances. Some experiments are being made with it, and its durability and racing power will soon be thoroughly tested. If it meets all the demands upon it we shall ere long see bam- boo cycles swarming on every highway.

Samuel Rogers, writing to his sister in 1834 on the fall of the Grey Ministry, said such a thing had never happened before as that a Cabinet Minister (Lord Holland) had first to learn he was out of office through the columns of a newspaper. Many diaries of those days refer to the same thing, but it is a wonder no longer. The enterprise of the Press outruns the official telegrams and despatches, and gets the news before it has reached the nearest -relatives. Mr. Gladstone learnt that his son (William Henry) was deal from the placards of the papers. An aristocratic bridegroom saw in the papers he was to be married on such a day. He wrote to deny it, but the Press was right, and had the bride's decision before he had. A few years ago an Eng- lish diplomatist was accused of sending news to the French generals in the war of 1870, when it ap- pearei that the information about the enemy, who were less than forty miles away, had reached them through the English Press.

A fortnight since the Press was five hours ahead of the Government as to the death of the Czar. But surely if there is a Chinese Samuel Rogers, he will record as a marvel of marvels that news about the enemy at the very gates of China bad to be waited for till the arrival of the English newspapers. So writes an English resident in the Flowery Land.

it will surprise and disappoint many well-wishers of Ireland to learn that there has recently been observable a marked growth of Fenian activity in this country. So far it has been limited to the larger towns, and particularly to London and Liver- pool. The police have various theories to account for this ; but it seems to be due very much to the impatience of the American section of the Irish party. Whether they may turn their thoughts chiefly against the House of Lords and translate them into action I cannot say. They have not up to the present shown any inclination towards vio- lent acts, but are endeavouring to force the running by more peaceful methods. It is this increase in Fenianism which has caused the police to put on a guard for the benefit of Mr. Morley, who, I believe, rather resents their meddling. Mr. Balfour has still a bodyguard furnished him when the police think it advisable.

I am informed from Windsor that at the banquet on Monday night in honour of the Czar's wedding the Queen looked very radiant. She is now allied to most of the Courts of Europe through her chil- dren and grand-children, end she was resolute to emphasise the occasion by standing up—a thing unprecedented—in proposing the health of " the bride and bride,room "—not the "Czar and Czarina "—es has been stated. Lord Cork took the place of Lord Carrington in proposing the Queen's health. It was very late when the Queen retired to rest, b,.calise she wirticularly desired a late tele- gram from Princess Alix, which only arrived on Tuesday morning.

The Chinese colony in London has been in a much more depressed frame of mind since the fall of Port Arthur than at any time since the Japanese began their march of victory. As for the Legation itself, it is now absolutely despondent, and the optimism which at first, despite all reverses, possessed it, has now fled. Its one hope seems to be in the Great Powers, who, however, are manifesting no particu- lar desire to interfere at present. though they may have something to say when the terms of peace come to be adjusted. Everything will depend on whether Japan i, moderate and reasonabh-Nin her demands, or whether she assumes the usual arrog- ance of a conqueror under the stimulus of her triumphs. Failing the intervention of the Great Powers Chinese residents here confess that Cl.ina is alrea:lv__beaten, and that she has no alternative but to rnftkii pence. But if the Ant condition of peace put forward by the Japanese is the occupa- tion of Pekin, such an occupation might lead to the sa consequences calculated riously to affect the interests of the European Powers in the East: and if her present misfortunes stir up China to throw off for the future her isolation and her hostility to European ideas, it is almost certain that when she has framed her military organisation upon 'European models the events which are now hapnen- ing will bequeath to the future a legacy of venge- ance which will not fail to be exacted.

At an Assize trial the other day a proseentrix, a tender creature of some eighty winters—who had brought an unfounded tharge of impropriety against a young fellow of six-and-twenty—was asked if she saw "the prisoner at the bar" at fl certain grocer-publican's. " No, sir ; but I saw him in the taproom!"

• Y.11.C.A. CHORAL SOCIETY

ANNUAL CONCERT.

There was a very good attendance at the Co- operative Hall on Monday night when the annual concert in connection with the Y.M.C.A. Choral society was held. The evening's programme was divided into two parts, the first of which was com- posed of Focer's popular cantata, "The Crusader," and the second was a miscellaneous entertainment. In the cantata the characters were as follows : Margaret (in love with Christ,an), Miss Kent ; Zara (a Sorceress among the Turks), Miss Fieldhouse ; Christian (a volunteer), Mr. Griffiths and Mr. Lloyd ; sentry. Mr. H. Booth ; Peter the Hermit (leader of the Crusaders), Mr. P. I. Bate, and a chorus of Crusaders and Turks composed of a number of male and female members of the society. Miss Kent, who is always a favourite with a Warrington audience was particularly successful in her rendition of the solo and alternate parts as Margaret, in the cantata. Mr. P. I. Bate was intrh appreciated as Peter the Hermit and his voice was heard to good ad- vantage,especially in his interpretation of the solo parts, "Without Jerusalem's Grey Walls," and, "No more the dark Invader," Miss Fieldhouse and Mr. Griffiths were also deserving of special note for the successful way in which they took their parts. Fault could hardly be found in the way the chorus was taken up, the alternate singing in which reflected great credit on their able cnnduetor, the Rev. E. Moore, G.T.C. After a few minutes' interval the miscellaneous programme was commenced by the part song, "Queen of the Night," by the choir. The other songs, &c., were rendered in a skilful and pleasing manner by the follow- ing :—Soßgs : Miss Kent, " Tit for Tat," Mr. Fieldhouse, and choir " The S:ngers," Miss Fieldhouse, "The land of Yesterday," Mr. Griffiths, "The Sailor's Grave,"the choir, " Zephyr taking thy repose." A solo violin en- titled "Suite of Waltses," as rendered by Mr. T. Booth- royd who responded to an encore, a flute solo, " Kul larnev," Mr. W. H. Podinnre and a duet entitled " Love and War," was sung by Messrs.Griffiths and Fieldhouse, Miss J. E. Pigot acted as pianist and the harmonium accompaniments were paved by Mr. F. Hickman. The singing of the National Anthem terminated the procerd- jags.

R UNCO RN.

Runcorn public hall was crowded on Friday evening on the occasion of the animal concert and prize distribution in connection with the 2nd V.B. Cheshire Regiment (F. and G. Companies). Miss Barclay, a lady well-known in Runcorn, presented the prizes, and the concert was provided by several well-known artistes viz: Miss Kate Drew (soprano), Miss Violet Simpson (contralto) Mr. Charles Chilley, and Mr. W. Pagan (humorist). Mr. Chilley contributed three items to the program:la-, and twice did he receive encores. " Love's Nocturne " (Laurence Kelly) was hie best effort, and as a matter or course he received voci. ferous applause. Complying with a recall he sang "In. the Bay of Biscay" which further delighted the audience " The Land of Yesterday" (Angelo Mascheroni) by Miss Drew was in every way a success and in this song she , was encored. Her other contributions were " 0 Rlses red and Beses White" (H. Young), and "Page's song from the Huguenots" (Meyerbeer). Miss Violet ;simpson is the possessor of a fine contralto voice and she sang well. Both "By the Fountain," and " The Bells of Ssville," were well suited to her style, and she was suc- cessful in securing a couple of encores. Humrorous sketches were given by Mr. William Pagan, and his rendering of the comic songs, " Three girls in a boat," and, " Mrs. Eory 'Awkins married," received a vocifer- ous applause and recalls. Mr. Birkett Musgrove was accompanist for the evening. The Volunteer Band, under the con.luctorship of Mr. Outhwaite excellently performed " Zar und Zimmermann " (Lortzin; and the fantasia," Storthing of Tel-El-Kebir." During an interval Miss Barclay distributed the prizes to the successful volunteers of the corps during the year. There were also on the Platform : Colonel Ashton, Cap- tain Dutton, Captain Hairison, Captain D. Ashton, Cap- tain Adjutant Ashton, Captain G. F. Ashton ; Lieuts. Evans and Brundrit, Sergeant Major Thomas, Quarter- master Sergeant Flankinson, Colour-Sergeants Poole and Mort; Sergeants Harvey, Woodland, Rushton, Holmes, Joynson, Edwards, Lea, Gittens, and Niel& Captain John Ashton said it was his pleasing duty ts appear before them once again in the capacity of commander of the Runcorn volunteers. It was a posi- tion of which he was very proud, and he thought any man ought to be proud of the position. He might say, that taking the year all round, they had increased in strength and efficiency. Not only in efficiency for drill, but efficiency for firing. He was very proud of that. They had increased in strength 4 per cent. ; in recruits, 4 per cent. ; and in efficiency, 3 per cent. ; and their percentage in camp had increased 5 per cent. The average attendance at drills showed an increase of something like 4 per cent., and, as regarded shooting, F Company had increased nearly 10 per cent. The other company (G) had not quite increased their percentage in shooting to the same extent. Out of 150 men they had got 33 marksmen who were first-class shots. (Applause.) F Company were the winners of the musketry cup for the year, G Company won the prize for the best tent ; and F company for the best line of tents. They had four veterans who were entitled to long service medals for being efficient volunteers for over twenty years. (Applause.) They were Quartermaster-Sergeant Han- kinson, Colour-Sergeant Mort, Colour-Sergeant Poole, and Bugler Buckley,Sergeant Harvey,who had wen Lady Brookes' cup for the third time, and who also won the gold medal out-and-out last year, had again won the gold medal this year. RUNCORN WESLEY kNS AND

TEMPER,A.NCE.

Sunday was observed in all the Wesleyan churches in the Rancor-4 circuit as Temperance Sunday. Special temperance sermons and ad- dresses were delivered in both churches and schools, and as a result a good number of Sunday school scholars were led to sign the pled7e. In St. Paul's Church, the preacher at the morning service was the Rev. T. Brackenhury ; at Halton-road Church, Mr. Field- ing, a student, fulfilled the duties for the Rey. W. George, who was indisposed ; and the Rev. W. Lilo Stone was the preacher at Halton Chapel. At Bruns- wick, Mill Brow, and Camden Sunday schools addresses on temperance were delivered by Messrs. J. D. Jones, Bishrown, and Fielding. On Monday evening a public temperance meeting was : held im St. Paul's Wesleyan Chapel, when there was a good attenrlance. Mr. 'l'. Simister occupied the chair, and there were also on the platform : Revs. T. Bracken- bury, W. George, and J. Lile Stone; Messrs. F. J. Nor- man„T.P., J. Simpson, E. Marshall, J. D. Jones, S. Terpperley, J. D. Bisbrown, J. Baguley, and G. Bellamy.

Mr. J. D. Jones (secretary) in submitting his report said that the figures he was about to read in no way described the temperance sentiment amone.st the Methodists either as related to Great Britain. the Liverpool district, or Runcorn district. 'I here was nothing more misleading than figures. The figures did not represent the strength and power of their temperance men and temperance week. They had 4,222 hands of hope with 435.141 members of the same, being an increase of 187 hands of hope, and 14,253 in membership. He had also to report 1,153 temperance societies with a membership of 68,798 beino, an increase of 169 societies and 7,622 !ti membership. The total in- crease for the last three years was 508 hands of hope and 58, 601 in membership, and an increase ef 381 temper- ance societies, and 17,253 in membership. In the Liver- pool district there were 221 hands of hope with a mem- bership of 28,375. There were also 46 temperance societies with a membership of 4,049. He had been un- able to get the figures for the hands of hope in that cir- cuit, but th.ro was one temnerancr society with a mem- bership of 404. (Applause); The 'Chairman said that meeting declared plainly what the attitude of the Runcorn circuit was respecting , the great question before them, viz. : total abstinence. There was no uncertain sound in their churches or Sun- day schools the previous day in reference to the temper- ance question. If they could get all their Sunday school teachers and Sunday school scholars to turn to this matter and resolve that they would abstain entirely

===== 0002601_18941201_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
E XAMIN

from all jot pci3tti ag drialts, the b ),,n to them Nould priceless, and would be a blessing that could not be des- cribed. Of course let them try all they could to shut up the public houses on the Sabbath day, and they would also be very glad to see the Local Option Bill passed. (Applause). He remarked that the sights which they saw in their streets ought to be enough to aff .ct every right thinking person in their midst. Rev. W. George said there was a great temperance work remaining to be done, and that work had to be done by the Church of Christ. By meeting together as they did that night they had their duty put clearly before them. Their conscience was aroused, their sym- pathy was awakened, and they gave encouragement to each other. After referring to the progress of the tem- perance cause in Methodism, he said they had the hope of the future. In their Methodist colleges it was very' difficult to find a man who was not an abstainer from alcoholic doinks. (Elem.. hear.) It was often said that temperance speakers made things appear worse than they were. They had the credit of being guilty of ex- aggeration. But they could not exaggerate in this matter. The evils were so great that they could not find colours black enough to paint them. There were those who still sneered at the Nonconformist conscience. But the Nonconformist conscience had to be reckoned with, and would have to be in the future more than in the past. They were not cyphers in the political life of this country. They counted for something both in Parliain ;a..y :nunicipal life. Politicians were not likely te help them very much unless they had the people bohind them. Behind all the temperance activities of which they heard so much,they might see the Church of Christ standing. (Applause.) rhe Rev. T. Brackenbury referred to ale relation of Methodism to the temperance cause. He said the Methodist church was a temperance church, and that temperance work was part of their church organisation. About 90 per cent, of their Methodist ministers were total abstainers. He thought every Sunday school teacher might take to the class a temperance pledge book, and endeavour to induce the sympathies of every scholar on the side of temperance. They had 130,000 Sunday school teachers and officers and these might be agents of Christian temperance. They had also nearly one million scholars, and this was a fine field for teinner- ance labour. Every class leader might occasionally talk to the members about the duty of uniting themselves for the good of others in this temperance cause. (Applause.) The Rev. W. Life Stone in a lengthy address, spoke of his work in the temperance cause, more especially of his endeavours to reform those who had become drunk- ards. He said they wanted work, not merely talk. They not only wanted fresh legislation, but they wanted also the present laws better administered. He was a Local Veto man, but would be willing to try the Gothenberg system. He would like to see public-houses closed earlier on Saturday nights, and also on Sunday. In conclusion, he said it was the duty of the Christian Church to blot out this great evil. Mr. .1. Simpson pointed out the importance of people being consistent when they professed to be teetotalers, and urged upon them to be steadfast in the work for the benefit of the young. Mr. F. J. Norman, in moving a vote of thanks to the Chairman, spoke of the progress the temperance cause had made, but pointed out the great danger of teetotal- ers becoming intolerant as was the case with some suc- cessful parties.—Mr. Bisbrown seconded the motion, which was carried unanimously. The meeting closed with the singing of the Doxology.

CRUELTY TO CHILDREN. --- A GOLBORNE CASE.-DISGUSTING

REVELATIONS.

On Saturday morning at the Newton Petty Sessions before Mr. T. J. Gillespie (in the chair), and other magistrates, Thomas Travis was charged with having on the 13th October neglected his children in such a manner as to cause injury tge their health.—Mr. Knowles, solicitor, who prose- cuted on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C., said the facts of the case were that the defendant Travis kept a house in Golborne, with his wife. Tie had eight children, six of whom were small and victims of the neglect and other two earning money. The defend- ant himself earned 353. a week, one son 245. and still another 16a., making a total of over £3 per week coming into the house. The furniture consisted of three chairs, one table and an mid sofa below' and upstairs there were ' two iron bedsteads one in each room, with a mattress and one sheet on them, while in a back room there was a large wooden bucket which was used as a closet, the steneh from which was abominable. The youngest child, six manths old, was clothed in a few rags satur- ated with urine and caked with excrement. Mrs. Travis was in Walton gaol and the six children had been in the Warrington Union until defendant took them out a short time ago. Most of the money which has come into the house has been spent by the male defendant and his wife in drink. While in receipt of such a good income they neglected the six children and lived a life like pigs in dirty and filthy surroundings.—lnspector McAteer, Parker-street, Warrington, said that on the sth of the said month he, in company with P.C. Simpson. of Golborne, visited the. defendant's house, 75, Chureh-st., Golborne, where they found his wife, Elizabeth Travis, I and six children in the kitchen. All the children were in a filthy condition. The eldest boy, Peter, aged 10. wore a pair of old pants very much torn and he had neither boots,stockings nor shirt on. His hair was long, matted together and filled with vermin and dirt. Peter, aged 8, was worse, and had scarcely anything to cover his nakedness, while his hair was in the same condition and he was crusted with dirt and did not appear to have been washed for a considerable time. Mary, aged six, was dressed with one frock and a portion of a tattered chemise, whilst her hair was also matted together, with her head covered with scabs, which the vermin fed upon. Thomas,four, was dressed with two froeks and had no shirt, and his body an rags were swarming with vermin. Bridget, aged two, was sitting in a corner with a chemise on in a fearfully dirty and filthy condition. The baby, Elizabeth, aged six months, was in the condition descrihod be Mr. Knowles. The woman Travis was under the influence of drink,while the kitchen was covered with cinders and other dirt. There was no food ;n the house for the family. The Inspector added he had never seen a worse case since he had joined the Society. Re visited the house with the same constable on the 13th of the same month, and found the children in the same condi- tion, and Mrs. Travis very drunk. When they snoke to her she took up one of the chairs and dashed it to pieces on the floor.—P.C. Blundell, stationed at Golborne, said he had known the defendant two years. He had been inside the house and found it in the condicion described by the Inspector, whose statement was quite correct. Dr. Part deposed to seeing the de- fendant almost dead drunk on the 6th October, with a hole in his forehead, caused by a kick. He had also visited the defendants' house with P.C. Simnson and found the children swarming with body lice. Their con- dition was likely to cause them serious injury.—William Travis, son of the defendant, said he was a collier,work- ing at Wigan Junction. He said their condition was his mother's fault, and not defendant's.—The Bench seemed to agree with him that it was more the woman's fault than the man's, who was away from home most of the time. They would, however, fine him 20s. and costs (£ll- 134. 61. in all), and said they hoped to deal with the woman when her two months' imprisonment elapsed.

WHAT AN INTERVIEW Pi.

A LESSON ON JOURNALDnI.

The interview is a modern contrivance, invented with the object of enabling a newspaper to ascertain exact facts at first hand, without possibility of error. An accredited representative of the newspaper calls at the house of anyone who has a remarkable experience to relate, and takes down an exact statement of the case. For instance, here is a very good example of an " Inter- view" from the "Bridgewater Mercury," a well-known and powerful newspaper. _ _

_ A reporter of the " Mercury" recently interviewed Mrs. Wheadon, of Boroughbridge, near Bridgewater, a very intelligent lady, who expressed herself perfectly willing to give him the information he wanted, hoping that it might be the means of doing good. "My little girl, Emily," she said, "is now in her ninth year. Ever since she was vaccinatad at eight months old, she had been a very delicate child, and given us no end of trouble. I had three doctors for her and they said she was suffering from consumption of the bowels and enlarged glands. The doctors thought she would never recover. She had no appetite and no colour, and was always tired. We tried all kinds of remedies. One day I happened to read in a newspaper about a child who lay near to death—its. death indeed hourly expected—but who had be m. saved at the last moment, when all hope was abandoned, by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale I:',ople, and I determined to give them a trial. To my great delight, in three days the child's health had visibly improved. Her appetite increased, her cheeks got a colour, and she became as lively and play- ful as other children. She is a rosy-cheeked little maid now, and I have not had a doctor for her since. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills saved my little girl's life." Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People are a perfect blood builder and nerve restorer, curing such diseases as rheumatism, sciatica, neuralgia, partial paralsis, loco- motor ataxy, St. Vitus' dance, nervous headache, nervous prostration,and the tired feeling resulting there- from, diseases depending upon humours in the blood, such as scrofula, erysipelas, &c. They are not a purga- tive, like other pills, and may be safely used by the most delicate ; they restore pale and sallow complexions to the glow of health, and are a specific for the troubles of the female sex, while in the case of men they effect a, radical cure in all cases arising from menial worry, over- work, or excesses of whatever nature.

These Pills are manufactured by the Dr. Williams' Medicine Company, of 48 Holborn-viaduct, London, and are sold by chemists everywhere, but it is essential that the true remedy he secured, and to do this the full name "Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People" must be seen on the pink wrapper. Pills sold loose or by the dozen or hundred are not of Dr. Williams' formula, which cannot be imitated or reproduced by retailers. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills are sold at 2s. 91 a box, or six boxes for 13s. 9d.. and may be had direct by post from the Com- pany at these prices.

THE NEWTON DISTRICT COUNCILS.

WARGRAVE WARD.

A. meeting in connection with the candidature of Messrs. Davies and Pryor was held in the Vulcan schoolroom on Tuesday evening. Mr. J. Rodgers presided, and there was a very fair attendance of electors. The chairman said he was pleased to be present in support of Messrs. Davies and Pryor, and in supporting them he did so for the reason that the promoters of the Parish Councils Bill used as an argu- ment in support of that bill, that they wanted to incul- cate a stronger interest, and a stronger desire in the working classes to take an active interest in working out the social welfare of the district in which they resided. That undoubtedly was the intention of the promoters of the measure and those who assisted in the passing of it. Obstacles were thrown in the way of the passing of the measure, but having overcome those difficulties, they would be ungrateful to those who had supported the measure, if they as working men were to sit quietly by and simply record their votes without taking any interest in the social welfare of the neighbourhood. Why should they not go ahead and aripreciate the rights they were getting, and use them, not to the exclusion of this or that party, but to the benefit of the people generally ? Mr. W. Davies said during his connection with Newton he had taken an active interest in the affairs of the neighbourhood, and in connection with the various associations in the district he had held as many posi- tions as anyone in the neighbourhood, and he did not think he had ever misused any of those positions, and on those grounds he had some reason, he thought, to COMP before them, and ask them for their votes on that occa- sion. It was his strong wish and desire to have work- ing men of all shades of opinion ,sn the District Councils, and he thought both the Councils and the neighbour- hood would be benefited by that, (Rear, hear.) Refer- ring to the address of Dr. Mouncey particularly with regard to the Cemetery question, he said that was a very important question. He was struck with the very heavy expenditure that fell upon the working-men as a class, in a case of bereavement, which was a time when working-men could very ill spare the money, and he found that the charge at Newton Cemetery was four times as much as at St. Helens. There were plenty of things in connection with the Cemetery that required reform, and if returned to the Council, he should always do his best to make the charge easy for his own class in case of bereavement. The night-soil was a very difficult question to deal with, but something would have to be ' done with regard twit in the district. Alluding to the increase in rates, he gave as his opinion that one way of meeting any increase was the taxation of ground rents. Whether he and his colleague were returned or, not, he hoped the time would sh )rtly arrive when they would have a fair representation of working-men on the Council. If they thought they would be best repre- sented by the three large employers of labour. he said vote for them and send them to the Council.— Mr. E. C. Pryor also addressed the meeting. He had nothing personal to say against any of the candidates as he believed they were all estimable men, but it was certain all six of them could not get on to the Council. He alluded to the sanitary condition of the Vulcan cottages, which he said reeinired looking into. One of the candidates, who was now a member of the Commis- sioners, out of 84- meetings had only attended 25, and was absent 59 times.—Mr. Cordingley moved a resolution pledging the meeting to do all in its power to return Messrs. Davies and Pryor at the head of the poll.—Mr. W. Jackson seconded and the motion was carried with two dissentients.—& vote of thanks to the Chairman closed the meeting.

'ft RDA Y DECEMBER 1 1894.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT WARRINGTON.

Thomas Troy, who has been so much trouble to the police in Warrington, by his inordinate desire to possess things which do not belong to him, and by his weakness for stabbing his brother with a penknife, etc. will not fall into their clutches again for a long time. His escapade with his brother caused his appearance at Liverpool Assizes before Mr. Justice Wills, with the result that he will be incarcerated in one of Her Majesty's prisons for the long term of five years. He will be a welcome riddance to Warrington, and frequenters of the police court will miss a well known face.

' Balaclava heroes are gradually dwindling away, and soon they will all be gone. One of them in the person of Sergeant Donoghue of Penketh passed away this week, and to some extent he was pre- eminent among them, for he sounded the charge of the bight Brigade. Our government are not over generous to the veterans who have risked their lives on behalf of their country, and although Sergeant Donoghue served both in the Crimea and in the Indian Mutiny, he only received the paltry pension of Bd. per day. This is poor remuneration indeed. In fact some of the survivors of the famous charge are at present in workhouses. Surely so long as England possesses an army, those who have served faithfully and well, ought •to receive better treatment than this.

' The oddfellows of the "Admiral Nelson" lodge have good cause to congratulate themselves,seeing that they had such a successful gathering at the Queen's Hotel on Monday night last week. The meet- ing: was honoured, not only by a very large attend- ance, but by the presence of such " big guns" as G. M.Diprose (London), P.G.M., 11. Flowers and Bro R. Pierpoint, M.P. An able address was given by the Grand Master pointing out the very successful and flourishing condition of the Oddfellow lodges and particularly the "Admiral Nelson Lodge.

How many more disagreeable things is the pre- vailing bad trade going to be responsible for? The latest "bitter pill" is to be found in the abandon- ment of the scheme for the construction of the proposed railway from the Lincolnshire coast to Warrington. There doesn't seem to be any sugar coating round the "pill," either, unless the likely continuation of the scheme when trade mends,can be designated so. Three years have passed now, since the first sod for the proposed line was cut near Chesterfield and up to the present time that portion between Newark and Lincoln has been nearly com- pleted. Undoubtedly the loss to Warrington is great as the amount of trade we are thus deprived of is very great and the opportunity for the open- ing up of the country side in the Grappenh all and Snutsford directien is last. I rather fancy that any more new schemes in cnnnection with this good old town will be looked upon with a doubtful eye.now that this scheme has been abandoned, and, sad to say, the docks are not yet commenced, though one out of the stipulated three years is almost past.

The inhabitants of Penketh and Sankey, and in fact everybody who uses Penketh-lane for the pur- pose of traffic will hear with satisfaction that negotiations are almost concluded whereby the lane at Halton's Bridge will be raised and straightened. It is quite time that something was done there. If a furniture van goes down the incline, when it gets to the curve it completely blocks the way, and nothing can pass until it is out of the way. It is, therefore, a matter of congratulation that this road is to be improved.

The debate upon the House of Lords which was held under the auspices of the Warrington Debating Society, was of a very interesting nature from beginning to end. There was a great deal of interest taken in it by the public as was shown by the large number which attended. The debate began with a duel between Mr. Pierpoint, M.P., and Mr. A. H. Crosfield, after which the discussion was thrown open. Councillor Roberts made a very vigorous speech on Friday evening, and on Wednesday also when he took up the task of replying on behalf of the Liberals. All the other speakers also helped to keep up the interest of the debate. Mr. Diggle, as usual,appeared in the character of humorist, and his interjections at times were very, annoying to the speakers. But as everyone knows, Mr. Diggle is a privileged man.

Evidently there is no satisfying the demands of some people, for Annie Ryan, the well known " character" at the Police Court, though she was endowed with a month's hard labour applied for another 7 days. As far as could be seen there was no particular cause for her unreasonable demand there except that of flouting the bench and showing how little she cared for their punishment. "Annie's" determination to secure the coveted week was so great that she three times returned to the " dock" for the purpose of condemning the magistrates to purgatory and various other more or less undesirable places. The Chairman however, was disinclined to part with the valuable period and " Annie " had to submit to be taken down to the cells below.

Mr.Diggle keeps "pegging away" with his appeals in support of the Florence Mill hands, and he is evidently of opinion that he will win sympathy by his much speaking. He came to the Borough Court on Tuesday and made another appeal for them, and during the course of his interview with the magis- trates he rather startled the court by claiming re- lationship with the leader of the Diggleite party on the London School Board, and also with Mr. Diggle, the famous billiard player. He described the Diggles as a" rum lot." I notice that he intends to still further advocate the cause of the unemployed to-night, when he will dvliver a lecture at the old Masonic Rooms, Sankey-street, upon the poor laws, showing them to be" harsh, cruel, and inhuman."

Further evidence of the success of the Wycliffe P.S.A. was afforded in the crowded tea meeting last week. An !interesting and amusing meet- ing was held after the tea, at Which Mr. T. Morris, the Rev. J. Yon2e, and last, but by no means least, .Mr. Risque, of Manchester, took the most promin- ent parts. The latter gentleman was rather a sur- prise to the audience—but certainly a success. His address was very humorous, and kept the audience in a continual ripple of laughter. The Rev. J. Yonge had good cause to remark in his address upon the attendance ; in fact, the crush of people necessitated two sittings at the tables. This, how- ever, was not much regretted by the second batch of tea-drinkets, as it only increased their appetites and consequently their capacity for "putting things away

Some time ago, several cases, were heard at the Warrington Borough Court in which persons were fined for travelling old, broken4own horses across the country to Grimsby in order to be shipped to Antwerp and Rotterdam, presumably for human consumption. It was then stated that Warrington was a general meeting place for that class of per- sons, it being on the highway from the west to the east coast. 1 notice in a Grimsby newspaper, that two Russians and a German were summoned at the police court at that town for the same offence and were each pretty heavily fined. Some rather pecu- liar revelations came out at the trial, and thy were not very complimentary to the foreigners. The solicitor for the prosecution said that it was the practice of certain persons to buy knackers' horses and ship them for Antwerp because they produced a better price than when sold in England as dogs' or cats' meat. After this, lam sure that English- men travelling abroad should be careful about what they are eating.

The decision of the Local Government Board in favour of adding the Cheshire townships of the Warrington Union to the Runcorn Union is any- thing but agreeable news to the ratepayers of War- rington Union and to many of the inhabitants of the townships. It was clear from the evidence given at the recent inquiry that there is a pretty strong feeling in Grappenhall, Thel- Ns, all, and Latchford (Without) in favour of the old love rather than the new, and it may be taken for granted that the foeling is not least strong amongst the poor peo,ple who are more or less dependent on Union relief. However, there is little to be gained by crying over the inevitable, and I suppose the decision of the Local Government Board is final.

"BUCKET-SHOP REVELA.TIONS."

Harry Meeking, aged 20, described as a compositor, pleaded guilty at the Cld Bailey to obtaining money by false pretences. The prisoner, it was adeged, carried on business as the City and Provincial Investment Corpora- tion, Westford and Company, Thomas Carson and Com- pany, and as the United Kingdom Stock and Share Brokers' Association. He had scattered circulars re commending various farms of Stock Exchange specula tion throughout the kingdom. A large number of people were defrauded.—Mr. Geoghegan, who defended said it was prepsterous to suppose that the circulars specimens of which• were before the court, could have emanated from the brain of the prisoner, who, although represented as speculating in hundreds of thousands of pounds, was in reality occupying two rooms at 3s. 3J. per week, and was positively at the time of arrest pur- suing the humble advocation of hawking vegetables about the streets.—The Common Serjeant said he would give the prisoner an opportunity of informing Inspector Downs or Detectives Dowse and Oats, who had charge of the case, of the names of his principals who had been plundering the public pf many hundreds of thousands fo pounds.—Sentence was postponed.

ELECTRIC LIGHT AND POWER. LANCASHIRE "A GARDEN OF EDEN."

There was a large attendance at the second meet- ing of the Literary and Philosophical Society on Mon day:evening last week in the Museum Lecture-room, The chair was occupied by the President (the Rev. L. J. T. Darwell). Mr. Arthur H. Crosfield, Walton Lea, and Mr. John Bernard, Sunny-side, Latchford were unanimously elected members of the society Other names were submitted, and they will be balloted for at the next meeting. The PRESIDENT, before introducing the Lecturer, remarked that four years had elapsed since a paper was I read before the society on electricity, and that was in connection with the telegraph ; therefore that evening they would have special interest in listening to Mr. Bally, because the subject was one which had the deepest concern for them all. He had no doubt that the lecturer, whose fame had preceded him, would greatly interest them. (Applause.) Mr. F. G. BAILY, M.A. (Lecturer in University Col- lege, Liverpool), then proceeded to read a paper on "The Modern D3velopments of Electric Light and Power," with experimental and 'lantern illustrations. Councillor F. W. Monks was in charge of the lantern. Mr. Baily remarked at the outset that the present age had been called generally the age of steam, and if one looked round in Lancashire and all parts of the North of England he would be quite convinced of the truth of the saying. Pretty nearly everything was done by steam and all the sdurces of their power were practi- cally summed up in the words "steam engine." There were a few exceptions perhaps, the gas engine being the most important, but still they could say that the age was essentially that of the steam engine. But there were considerable signs that the subsequent ages, the next immediate time would be ta) a much larger extent not the age of steam,but the age of coal transferred into elec- trical power, and that electricity would be the immedi- ate source of all their power in their everyday life. Even at present the various ways in which electricity was applied was one of the most surprising occurrences of the latter end of the century. There was hardly any branch of life to which electricity had not been applied, although the application was only to a limited extent. In most large towns electric lighting was an accom- plished fact, and there was to be seen street lighting, lighting in houses, and all sorts of artificial lighting, down to microscopic slides and illuminating for medical purposes. Heating was thus also done, although to a small extent, with its various applications to cooking and so on. The transmission of power, the carrying of power from one place to another, had received an enormous improvement,notably in the case of the Niagara scheme, and to a limited extent it had been applied in England. There were also wide possibilities in the way of smelting and forging and welding, and nearly all the copper was now purified by electricity. It had a wide application in the medical interest, part of which was of doubtful utility, whilst other applications were based on a thoroughly sound scientific basis, and the latest use to which electricity had been applied was with regard to the transmission of signals, but it would take up too much time to ' attempt to deal with all of them that evening. He would, therefore, limit himself to the application of electricity te lighting and to the transmission of power, they being the most important ones at the present time, and for a long time to come. Although he had said that the next age would be one of electricity,they would never get rid in England at all events of the coal fields; they could never rely on anything but coal for a source of power, simply because they had no other source. One of the great functions of electricity was to enable them' to transmit power from one place to another with great ease. They were familiar with many instances—the ordinary belt from a fly wbeel to a pulley, and the cable tramway, and the steam and water pipe were all examples of the trans- mission of power; but the great advantage of electricity was to simplify the transmission of power very much. It gave them a very much wider range of distance, and a wider range of application at the other end. A num- ber of lantern illustrations were then thrown on the screen, and Mr. Baily also gave a number of successful experiments of an interesting character. In conclusion, he said they could expect a large extension of these various applications in a few years' time, as soon as people more fully appreciated the benefits to be obtained from electricity, both in electric lighting and in power. One could hardly understand why it was not more gen- erally adopted. The electric light was not only efficient, but clean, safe, and cooler than gas or oil, and it did not make the smallest amount of impurity in the air; in fact it supplied a little oxygen. They could hardly over- estimate the benefits of electicity in a crowded office. Electricity was really one of the moat hopeful means of getting rid of the appalling trouble of smoke. It was almost a disgrace to the science of the country that it had not done something material to get rid o!it,whereas as a matter of fact nothing had been done. - One could abolish seven-tenths of the smoke by the use of electric- ity and good stoking; it would make Lancashire a gar- dee of Eden if electricity were more generally used. (Applause.)

Mr. BROADBENT proposed and Mr. A. J. Jolley seconded a vote of thanks to Mr. Baily for his interesting lecture. —Mr. William Long,in supporting the motion, said that those who were most familiar with electricity Pp to this point felt that there was a large unknown future before it. It would be a great boon to all of them if they could impress upon their manufacturers the fact that they could do their work without poisoning the people. (Hear, hear, and laughter.)—Mr, .1. RYMER YOUNG also spoke, and the motion was then put to the meeting and heartily agreed to.
