TfiE EX,A.M.;NER,

SATURDAY, DECEMBER 1, 1884.

WITHIN a few days, almost the The Elections. whole country will be in the midst of an unex am pled series of elections. No fewer than about sev en thousand separate parishes will each elect its parish council, and in six thousand other and smaller parishe s parish meetings will be held. Then in every dis- trict which is at present governed in its local affairs by a Board of Commissioners or a Local Board an election will be held of the district councillors who will take over the business of the Commissioners and the Local Boards. It is estimated by the President of the L ocal Govern- ment Board (Hr. SHAW LEFEVRE, M.P.), that, altogether, there will be, within a brief period, no fewer than about twenty-three thowsand elections, and some ninety thousand persons will be elected in one capacity or another. And all this will be the result of the recent Local Gov ernmentAot, familiarly spoken of as the" Parish Councils Bills" Truly, in the face of these figures it can only be described as a great act of enfranchisement. The importance of the changes which this great measure is destined to make in the public life of England—and particul any in the rural parts of England can be no no longer gainsaid. Apathy is giving place to the liveliest interest, and even the poorest of labourers and the smallest of householders are beginning to realise that they have a distinct and effective voice in all that concerns the social well-being of their own locality. These remarks apply with few ex- ception to the country at large, if one may judge from tbe reports that appear in the Press, and they certainly apply with especial force to most of the parishes &-urrounding Warrington. It is quite clear that the new Act is not to remain a dead letter in this part of the country. Th meetings that have been held all indicate that the situation has been fairly grasped, and, fortunately, there seems to be little disposition to disparage or dis- credit the Act or to throw obstacles in the way of its due fulfilment. This cannot be said of all parts a the country. From some of the more strictly rural districts there is a cry of clerical persecution, and in other places it is said that workmen are being discouraged—to use a mild phrase—by their employers from acting up to the full rights which have been conferred upon them. In one instance it is said that the Rector has intimated to the agricultural lalourers of his parish "that if they elected any candidates other than the landlords and farmers, and a rate was made, they were quite prepared to correspondingly raise the rents of the cottages to meet it." It is to be hoped that wherever ill-advised threats of this sort are made, the labourers will have the courage to disregard them. It is, of course, not always easy for men of this class to withstand pressure when it is put upon them by those who can, if they be unscrup- lous enough, deprive them of their employment and even of a place of habitation. But the future is with the labourer. Now that local government of a thoroughly democratic character has been given to rural districts, all the grosser forms of intimidation will become more and more difficult to practice. The parsons and the squires who have heretofore, in some cases, ruled with an al- most despotic sway will be under the necessity of trying more conciliatory methods.

THERE still 'wells to be some The, " Libera- doubt about the extradition of tor " Victims JABEZ BALFOUR, and even the Foreign Secretary, in reply to a correspond ent can express no decisive opinion as to when the "laws delays" of the Argentine Republic will finally come to an end. There has been a str ondesire to see BALFOUR again on English soil, and in the pre- sence of English kites. Whet her or not that desire will ever be gratified there seems to be really no means of saying. Argentine legal methods are unusually slow; they seem to be the very reverse of the . lynching processes which favoured in the more northern parts of the American continent. In fact, many people are firmly convinced that so long as BALFOUR can be used as a sort of conduit through which a golden stream can be made to flow into the pockets of officials and lawyers he will be retained in South America. •In the meantime, the money which is being squandered over this extradition might be much more usefully employed in relieving the necessities of the victims of the Liberator crash. In an appeal for aid which has been very influentially signed by Cabinet Ministers, peers, bishops, and eminent Nonconformist ministers, it is stated that ' the Liberator Relief Fund is being greatly hani- pored in its beneficent work by a falling oft in the contributions." Over £43,000 has been subscribed and nearly 2,200 sufferers have been temporarily helped, whilst a, i.ne 500 of the most destitute over seventy years of age have been permanently re- lieved, and will receive a substitute for their lost dividend as long as they live. But it seems that there are many hundreds of victims between sixty and seventy years of age who,af ter a laborious and thrifty life are now quite penniless, and, apart from the relief fund, have no prospect hut the workhouse. This is truly a pitiable end to be brought to by the wiles of unscrupulous and heartless speculators.

are I 1

ONE Of the manifold 11903 to which Electrocution, electricity is put in America is that of executing—or, as it is said electrocuting—criminals. To all appearances the powerful current of electricity which is turned Upon tile doomed indieiautor Cutlae.s death.

But, in the opinion of some people, appearances are, in this case, deceitful. It is said that although the " electrocuted " individual,when he is released from the apparatus on which he has been placed shows no signs of animation, he is not really dead and could be resuscitated by certain well understood methods. This is an allegation which can only be proved or disproved in one way, and that is by a practical test. But, even in America, they seem to shrink from applying the test. There are, it is easy to understand, some rather serious difficulties in the way. It is too much to expect that even the most devoted "martyr of science" will volun- teer his own body as the subject of the experi- metit. The risk is too great, and, besides, in the event of a fatal result, there is the case of the man who sets in motion the electric current to be con- sidered. What position would he hold in the eyes of the law? He could hardly be described as less than an accessory before the fact. The only way to meet the emergency was to apply the best to a condemned criminal. He would have nothing to lose and everything to gain by it. If resuscitated he would at least escape with his life, while if not resuscitated he would be in no worse plight than if no attempt at resuscitation had been made. It was accordingly decided to make the experiment at a forthcoming execution of a criminal in New "York and the Governor of, the State had given his sanction. But here again according to the lastest news, the scheme has mis- carried. Public opinion has been aroused against the project, and, over and above this, the law officers have declared it to be contrary to law. It is evident, therefore, that whether dead or only seemingly so, an electrocuted criminal will still be dealt with as though life was extinct.

THE Home Secretary, in reply to The Watch Coma letter from the Clerk to the War- mittee and the Magistrates. rington magistrates, says he has been advised that "in strict law" the Watch Com- mittee are competent to give instructions to the Chief Constable to lay before them all reports of offences against the licensing laws and to submit to their decision as to whether or no legal proceed- should be instituted. He adds, however, that "this is a matter in which great discretion is necessary in order to secure the due administra- tion of the law." The Watch Committee are en- titled to all the comfort they can extract from this letter. The question as to the policy or propriety of their action is left exactly where it was. When one is told that the Watch Committee intervene between the police and the magistrates in licens- ing offences the question at once arises. Why do they intervene? Do they intervene in a similar way in other cases—in cases, for instance, of theft or assault? And if not, why not? The one is just as much an offence against the law as the other. Apart altogether from what it may or may not do in "strict law," the Committee is clearly acting in a very injudicious and improper manner when it steps in between the police and the magistrates. The latter,sitting in open court, are indisputably the proper body to adjudicate upon all criminal charges.

NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN.

BY AN OCCASIONAL CORRESPONDENT.

The meeting at Haydock last week, called for the purpose of effecting some sort of an arrangement by which a contest in connection with the forth- coming elections to the District Council could be avoided, hardly turned out as the promoters ex- pected. In fact, I '.'ancy,the result came somewhat as a surprise, for to my mind everything seemed to be cut and dried, which is borne out by the state- ment made by Mr. Grimke-Drayton, that he ex- pected the meeting would not last more than half- an-hour. He said they wanted 12 candidates for the three wards, and as the nine members of the present Local Board were willing to be nominated, they would only want three other names. The meeting, however,wanted to have something to say on this, and there was a regular scramble for the doubtful honour, the Chairman in the end having to announce that 20 names had been handed in for 12 places, thus making it necftsary to have a con- test.

Speaking of the Chairman reminds me of a very nice point which Mr. Grimke-Drayton brought for- ward. When all were assembled it was seen there was no Chairman, and Mr. Drayton on getting on his feet said there was a little difficulty in the way of appointing one, for, according to the Act (al- though he admitted there was an element of doubt) anyone who presided at that meeting would be pre- cluded from becoming a candidate. or of voting at the election, and he was supported in this view by Mr. Ellis, solicitor. I must confess, I am not well up in the provisions of the new Act, but if what Mr. Drayton says is a fact, then I am afraid some of the candidates for the EarleAown Districi: Coun- cil have forfeited their rights, by presiding at meetings in connection with the coming election. I can hardly think, however, that this statement by Mr. Drayton can be correct, but if it is, what a handle for opponents to get hold of!

We are nearing the conchst period, when I expect to see a thinning in the numbers of candidates for Council honours. They have sprung up like mush- rooms, and if one might judge from the various canvasses which have been made,nll the eaadidates have an equal chance. Either the reports of the canvassers are not to be relied on, or the inhabit- ants have become so weary of seeing so many very excellent qualities paraded before their eyes, that they are glad to promise almost anything to get rid ot their importunate visitors. One candidate, who was about the first to solicit the suffrages of the ratepayers, has retired from action, and I shall be very much surprised, if he does not have a good sprinkling of followers. Up to now, however, I must confess, the contest, if such it can be called, has been carried on in a gentlemanly manner, without any of those hickerings, and trotting forth of personalittea which we sometimes see on such occasions as these.

I see the notices with regard to the elections have been posted,the nomination day being fixed for Wednesday next, December sth, and the elec- tion for Monday, December 17th.

THE RUNCORN COMMISSIONERS AND

THE GRAVING DOCK.

A special meeting to consider the proposals of the Manchester Ship Canal Company with regard to the proposed graving dock at Runcorn, was held in the Beard room on Tuesday evening. No reporters were admitted, but we are informed that Mr. Grice occupied the chair and that there were also present: Messrs. Leach, Bisbrown, Lea, Flankinson, Collier, Meadowcroft, Lightburn, Lake (law clerk), and Marshall (secretary). The proposals submitted by Mr. Collier to the General Purposes Committee were read by the Law Clerk and it was proposed by Mr. Bisbrown, seconded by Mr. Leach, and resolved that in view of the fact that the tenure of office by the Board of Commissioners i- so short they do not think it advisable to further pro coed with the matter until after the election of the new Urban District Council. A proposal from the Associated Shipwrights Society was brought forward, asking that a deputation might be received from the Society in order to state their views upon the matter. It was resolved that the Board would be pleased to receive a deputati.n from the Socivtv or from any other interested parties, hut as the Board had no intention to decide the matter in hand, the Society should make application to the new District Council, when formed, to receive a deputation.

Captain Joseph Shaw, 53 years of age, of 35, Low- lands-road, Runcorn, a well-known sea captain and Ship Canal pilot, died suddenly on Wednesday morning at Liverpool. The deceased having missed the last Crain from Liverpool to Runcorn on Tuesday night, went to lodge for the night at Mrs. Harrison's restaurant, Man Island, Liverpool. On going to bed he appeared in his usual health, but complained of tightness of the chest. Early next morning he was found dead in bed by the landlady, and death is attributed to heart disease. - Ti UTE MR. TIMPERLEY, OF RUNCORN. In Camden Wesleyan Chapel, Runcorn. on Sunday evening, the Rev. W. Temperley was 60 years of age, anl had resided in Runcorn all his life. From an apprentice he rose to be master, and starte4 in the busines 33 years since with which he was associated at the time of his death. For over a generation-36 years—he had been a member of the Methodist Free Churches, originally with the Free Church, but 27 years ago, up to the hour of his death, Camden was the centre of his active reli- gious effort. He had held the position of society stewari, was a class leader for 20 years,was the teacher of an adult class.was the treasurer of the sick club, and was a local preacher in the circuit. THE EXAMINER, sti TITRPAY, DECEMBER 1, 1894.

CHARGE OF STEALING SIXPENCE.

On Wednesday morning, before Mr. F. J. Norman and Mr. T. Handley, at the Runcorn Police Court,Julia Cole- m in, of Runcorn, was charged with stealing sixpence, the moneys of Emma Moss, Sea-lane, from the person of Isaac Mills. It was alleged against the defendant that when the child Isaac Mills, was returning with change from Mrs. MOSS, the defendant took the coin out of his hand. A difficulty was experienced in proving the case through the tender age of Mills, who is only six years of age, and who did not thoroughly understand the nature of an oath.—The magistrates accordingly disclihrged the defendant. THE CHEMICAL TRADE IN RUNCORN.

On Wednesday evening the notices in the United Alkali Company's Works at Runcorn, for the clesing of the works expired, and a large number of men are now out of employment. Some of the workmen, however, are still working, in order to finish some material in course of manufacture. These, it is expected, will be found employment until the end of next week, when the works will be practically closed. ST. JOHN'S PRESBYTRRIAN CHURCH, RUNCORN,

BRUNSWICK BAND OF HOPE. RUNC*RN,

A meeting was held in c4nnectlo'n with this society on Tuesday evening, when there was a large attend- ance. Mr. Posnett occupied the chair and delivered a short address. A programme of songs, readings, and recitations was gone through by the children, who were conducted by Miss C. Littler. The accompanist was Master J. D. Bisbrown. WIDNES WORKS DESTROYED BY FIRE.

Rally on Thursday morning the works of Messrs. J. Bibby and Sons, seed crushers, of Exchange-chambers, Tithebarn-street, Liverpool, which are situated in Elizabeth-street (close to the London and North-Western Railway) Widnes, were destroyed by fire. It appears that one of the workmen, not very experienced in the working of the place, was sent to get steam up, and while doing so he accidently let fall the lamp he was carrying into a quantity of fat. The result was that the grease at once became ignited. AppliaTices were at hand for extinguishing a fire, but the man appears not to have known how to use them, *lnd before assstance was forthcoming the whole place was in flames. A large body of police, under the direction of Superintendent K.-ighley,rendered valuable assistance in preventing the flames spreading to the adjenning property, and the fire brigade was summoned, but by the time of their arrival the flames had got such a firm hold of the works that it was impossible to save any portion of them, and the whole building was reduced to ruins in a very short space of time. Toe railway telegraph wires were broken, and the 6.55 train from Widnes was delayed twenty m notes. The damage (said to be tin overed bc insurance) is variously estimated at between £l,OOO and £1,500.

RUNCORN SCIENCE AND ART CLASSES.

DISTRIBUTION OF PRIZES.

In Greenway-road Board Schools on Thursday evening, Dr. McDougall distributed the prizes to the successful students of the science and art classes. There was a large attendance. Amongst those present were Messrs. H Pritchard, T. Handley, J.P., S. Pope, E. 'raison, F. Wiswall, Moulton, Bowyer, and J. Moss (clerk.) Prior to the distribution of prizes, the Chairman (Mr. Pritchard) called upon Mr. Moss to read his report. Mr. Moss (clerk) read the third annual report from which it appeared that the number of pupils attending classes in the past session, 1893-4, and the preceding ses- sion,lB92 3,showed a falling off of 100 in the total num- ber of pupils. This was accounted for by the discon- tinuance of the cookery class for want of suitable acco- modation and the abandonment of the classes in phy- siography, electricity, and chemistry owing to the teacher falling ill,shortly after the commencement of the session. The rest of the classes had been well attended, and had done good work. The following was a list of students, showing the certificates gained :—BUILDING CONSTRUCTION (advanced second class) : Wm. Lillis, H. Davies, Walter Wisvrall ; elementary pass, William Kershaw. D. Williams. MACHINE CONSTRUCTION (advanced 2nd class) : Jas. Steele, Geo. Williamson, John Wilson. Elementary pass, John Baker, John Williams, Wm. Cooper, El. Turton. Applied mechanics. advanced 2nd., Walter Wiswall, elementary pass, Percy Buckley. Steam, elementary pass, Walter Wiswall, E. J. Harrington. Freehand Drawing (advanced stage, Ist class) : Geo. Muss; elementary stage, Ist class, Arthur Poole; 2nd class, Eleanor Davies, Carrie Abel, Gerty Done, John Peacock. Model Drawing, (elementary stage, 2md class), Edward Rosser, John Peacock. Perspective, Ist class, George Moss ; 2nd class Harry Crowther, shading cast (advanced stage) 2nd class, George Moss. French, ele- mentary grade, (2nd class) : Ernest A. Ward, 3rd class, William Matthows, Edward W. Rosser. Shorthand (ele- mentary) passed, Henry G. Johnson, and Thomas Wes- ton. Speed, 3rd class, John D. Bisbrown. Mr. Moss said it was difficult to compare the above results with those of the previous session, as there had again been an alteration in the classification of passes by the science and Art Department, but on the whole the proportion of passes showed an improvement. The annual statement of accounts showed that ths amount drawn from the Runcorn CoRI missioners was £lBO. (Applause.) Mr. Pritchard, in asking Dr. McDougall to present the prizes and certificates, said the course they were pursu- ing that night in distributing prizes to those pupils who had gained certificates, was something like virtue, and brought its own reward. The awarding of prizes was an extra advantage given to the pupils. (Applause.) Dr. McDougall, alter presenting the prizes, which in- cluded several given by Messrs. Wilding, and J. Speak- mun, teachers of twine of the classes, said he was sure they would allow hum to convey their congratulations to those to whom he had had the honour of hand- ing the certificates and prizes they hai earned dur- ing the last session. He thought the advantages the pupils had now within their reach of gaining useful knowledge at so little cost, were conferring upon them benefits which neither their fathers nor forefathers had, and which were beyond all praise. Their Science and Art Committee would soon cease to exist, and the work would be taken over by a committee of the new District Council, and carried on on .a larger I scale. They had only been able to expend a modest £lBO, but their successors would have a much larger sum to expend. The speaker referred to the erection of the Technical Institute, and urged his hearers to take advantage of it. He did not like to see a young man standing at street corners with a cigarette in his mouth, when education like that was so easily within his reach. - -

Mr. Wiswall proposed a vote of thanks to Dr. Mc- Dougall for presenting the prizes.—Mr. S. Pope seconded, Mr. Imison supported, and it was carried with acclamation. A vote of thanks was also accorded the Chairman on the proposition of Dr. McDougall, seconded by Mr. T. Handley.

THE DISTRICT COUNCIL ELECTIONS AT RITNCO RN.

THE LIBERAL COUNCIL'S DECISION.

There is every probability of a contest taking place in each uf the six wards, into which Runcorn is divided, for the election of urban district coun- cillors. Three councillors are entitled to sit for each of the Halton, Mersey, Central, Bridgewater, Newtown, and Heath waads, and already more than eighteen candidates have entered the field. The last day for receiving nominations is Wednesday after- noon next, when they must be handed in to Mr. Lake, law clerk, at the Town Hall, not later than two o'clock. A very largely attended meeting of the Runcorn Liberal Council was livid on Tuesday evening, in the Trades' Hall, John-st. The business for the evening was the consideration of the replies of candidates to the questions submitted to them by the Liberal Council. Mr. Wm. Hill occupied the chair. After an animated dis- cussion it was ultimately decided to support the follow- ing gentlemen as candidates for the new district coun- cil :—Rev. J. Simpson (Congregationalist minister), Dr. Gastaldi, Dr. McK,mzie, Mr. Wm. Handley, Mr. A. R. Norman, Mr. P. Speakman 'Mr. J. D)bson, Mr. T. Lea, Mr. T. Reynolds, Mr. E. Speakman, (secretary WaterrAen's Union), Mr. J. Hayes Mr. D. Bisbrown, Mr.M.J.Sweet- man,Mr.T.Dean,Mr.J:H.Kirkham,and Mr.W .M. Williams. One other name is left over for further inquiry. All the above gentlemen have pledged themselves to the progres- sive programme arranged by the Liberal Council. A further meeting of the Council is to be held on December 6th, the day following the date of nominations, when the Council will decide upon what action to take. Of the other candidates who intend to go to the poll may be mentioned Mr. S. Taylor, J.P., Mr. Wiswall, Mr. Frith, Mr. Lightburn, Mr. Imison,and Mr. Collier. As far as can be ascertained the wards will be con- tested as follows : HEATH WARD: Messrs. P. Speakman, J.P., C.C., J. Pritchard, J. W. Lightburn, and G. Hayes. NEWTOWN WARD: Messrs. D. Bisbrown, E. Gatley, T. Reynolds, W. Leach, R. Abel. and W. M. Williams. Hat Torr WARD: Messrs. S. Taylor, J.P., F. Wiswall, J. R. Frith, and M. J. Sweetman. CENTRAL WARD: Messrs. A.R. Norman, T. Lea, and and G H. Kirkham. BRIDGEWATER WARD: Key. J. Simpson, Dr. Mc- Kenzie and Mr. T. Dean. MERSEY WARD, Rev. Dr. Gastaldi,Messrs. W. Handley, W. Hankinson and J. Florsfield. Mr. E. BODY FOUND IN THE CANAL.

On Tuesday morning the body of a man was found floating in the Sankey Canal, near the Ship Inn at Earlestown. Information was conveyed to P.C. R. Green, who took steps to have it recovered, and conveyed to the Ship Inn, where it now lies to await the inquest. The body was fully dressed, and on being searched papers were found indicat- ing that the man was named C. Grainger, and had carried on business as a tripe dresser at 110, Legh- street, Earlestown, and lodged with a Mrs.Nunner- ley, Regent-street. lie was a married man, and lived at Ulverston. The deceased man left Earles- town about a month ago with the intention of go- ing to Birkenhead, and this is the last that was seen of him. The body is in an advanced stage of decomposition, and has evidently been in the water since the day be left for Birkenhead, but has doubtless been fast in the mud, until released by a passing boat. NICELY CAIJOHT AT EARLESTOWN.

On Sunday at noon a man giving the name of William Harrison, Bridge-street, Rancorn, was ap- prehended on the back premises of Mr. J. Ice, grocer, Market-street, Earlestown. A man named Patrick Garland, who lives at 33, Grafton-street, who overlooks the first-mentioned premises, was in his bedropm when he saw the prisoner climb the backyard wall of Mr. Ince's shop, and drop over into the yard. Thinking he was after no good he went out taking the dog with him, and sent his wife for the police. The prisoner finding he was discovered hid himself under some old sacks and straw, where he was found by Inspector Morgan, who took him into custody on the charge of being on enclosed premises for an unlawful purpose. On being charged the prisoner said he was (trunk and did not know how he got into the yard. lie was brought up before Colonel Woid on Monday morn- ing and remanded to Warrington Sessions. ALLEGED COWARDLY THIEF. _ _

At the Town Hall, Newton, on Monday, before Colonel Wood, Joseph Naylor of Fairclough-st., Earlestown, was charged with stealing two geese from Holly House Farm, Newton, the property of Mr. Thomas Molyneux, and with assaulting an old man named Oliver, at the same place on t,aturday night. The man Oliver fastened a number of geese in the stable about six o'clock, and st 6.45 was going round the premises with a lamp, when he saw the prisoner with a goose in one hand, coming from the stable door. Oliver stopped him, when he dropped the goose, and struck and kicked the old man in a cowardly manner. Oliver's son-in-law named Austin came to his assistance, and together they secured Naylor, but after getting his name and address they released him, and gave informa- tion to the police, who arrested him the same night 'at 11.15. When Oliver returned to the stable he found another goose outside the door. The prisoner wa remanded to Warrington Sessions.

George Clarke, secre- tary, being responsible; and the varied questions and answers on the subject named were taken up by the members, and proved both an interesting and a profttable item. Sister R. Warburton impres- sively recited "Give me thy heart ;" Bro. John Taylor gave a melody, and Bro. John Miller also contributed a recitation in a satisfactory manner. Bro. Herbert Martin, who has recently returned from India, gave a very interesting sketch of the position and prospects of the Good Templar Order in that part of the world, for which he received thanks. A very enjoyable session was afterwards closed in the usual way.

UNITED TEMPERANCIC MEETING AT EARLBSTOWN.

On Wednesday evening a united temperance meeting wam held in the Wesleyan Lecture hall, under the presidency of Mr. Jno. Forster of St. Helens. There was a very fair attendance. After the meeting had been formally opened, the Chair- man said they had perhaps seen it in print that if they had a sensible reduction in the number of public-houses in this country, they would reduce the output, and the number of inebriates and in- temperate people, and in the same proportion lessen the number of their prisons and asylums. If there was no other way of convincing the publican and brewer that he was ruining families, he would saz make them into a Board of Governors of a county asylum The increase of asylums would continue until they took some drastic measures to put a stop to this evil of drink. (Applause.) The temperance question had more than held its own. The temperance party were making their mark upon the minds and intelligence of the community at large, ani he hoped that if they did their duty they would keep on making their mark until they had gained the victory. He alluded to the intro- duction of the Local Veto Bill, which he said had been brought in because the country demanded it, and until it was pasmed he hoped they would not be satisfied.—The Revs. J. Bartley and F. E. Miller also spoke, and the following resolution, moved by Mr. Graham, was unanimously adopted. " That this united temperanca meeting declares its keen dis- pleasure at the long postponement of the Liquor Traffic (Local Control) Bill, and, believing it to be of vital importance to the whole of the nation, earnestly implores Her Majesty's Government not to allow this measure to be shelved, but to bring it again before Parliament at an early period of the next Session."

ST. JOHN'S CHURCH, EARLESTOWN. In connection with the mott,er's meetings which have been instituted under the auspices of the above, a social tea meeting was held in St. John's Hall, on Thursday afternoon, about 40 being present. It has been decided to hold a social tea monthly, the object being to bring the women in closer contact, with each other. Address.s were given by the Revs. E. W. W. Kaye, and J. Andrews, the meeting being much enjoyed.

FORMATION OF A LIBERAL CLUB AT EARLESTOWN. _ _

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT RUNCORN.

(BY AN OCCASIONAL CONTRIBUTOR).

The Runcorn Commissioners—eight of them at least—were engaged on Tuesday evening in con- sidering tbe graving dock question. The proposals submitted by Mr. Collier to the General Purposes Committee were considered. But as to what those proppsals are the public of Runcorn are, as yet, in the dark. One would have thought that, with the near approach of the Urban District Council elec- tions, it would have been of advantage to the mem- bers of the old Board to submit to the public their proposals for dealing with this vital question affect- ing Runcorn trade. But it appears that the mem- bers are still bound to secrecy in this matter, and therefore very few " outsiders " are likely to know what is being done. The Commissioners decided after two hours' discussion on Tuesday, to postpone the consideration of the matter until the new Coun- cil is elected, so that the question will lie in abey- ance for some time.

An event worthy of notice, and especially inter- esting to Runcornians, occurred at Saltport on Wednesday. The vessel "Helene" was proceeding up the Ship Canal with a cargo of wood-pulp for Manchester, but at Saltport it was found that, in order for the vessel to proceed to its destination, it would be necessary to have the masts cut to enable it to pass under the various bridges. The captain, however, objected to disfiguring his vessel in this fashion, and declined to proceed further up the canal. The result was that the cargo has had to be discharged at Saltport, and a number of labourers have been busily engaged discharging since Wed- nesday. If this example is frequently followed it should mean a little more trade for Runcorn and district.

The Runcorn Liberal Council has now marshalled out it 3 army of candidates for the coming fight for the honours of Urban District Councillors. These candidates are pledged to the progressive pro- gramme which has been arranged by the Council, and, as will be seen, a number of workingmen can- didates are included in the number. One publican, in the person of Mr. J. M. Williams, of the Bridge- water Arms, is also included.

OUR LOCAL CHURCIIES-WARRINGTON.

ST. JOHN'S PRESBYTERIAN CHURCH.

At the above church on Stlndav the Rev. J. Warnock, delivered a discourse on "People whose ways are hedged in." He took as his text Job iii. 23, "Why is light given to a man whose way is hid, and whom God hath hedged in ?" There was a good attendance. As usual the rev. gentleman dealt with his subject in an able and interest- ing manner.

"STARTLING REVELATIONS OF INTEMPERANCE.", SERMON BY REV. D. W. ROBERTS.

The Golbornt-st. Biptist Chapel was again well filled on Sunday evening when the pastor (Rev.D.W. Roberts) preached a stirring sermon on the above subject. He said a startling revelation of these latter days of modern civilization was the fact t',at intemperaece was so pre- valent among wemen—not only among the lowest but also amone the better class. This was due in a great measure to the facilities given for secret drinking by the grocer's license act. Mr. Gladstone has said, "It has been said that greater calamities are inflicted on man- kind by intemperance than by the three great historical scourges, war, pestilence and famine. This is true and it is the measure of our discredit and disgrace." Yet added Mr. Roberts, the very man who said that passed into law the most cursed to women of all acts— the grocer's license Act. The harm done was incalculable. Women, servants and children of re- spectable households who could not or would not pro- cure intexicatine drinks at public houses were en- couraged to purchase and use these liquors by the opportunities offered when visiting grocers' shops for other purchases. This wag nothing more nor less than a direct incentive to secret drinking, a practice more in- jurious to health and morality and the social prosperity of the community than the ordinary trade in intoxicat- ing liquors. And those who thus drink almost inevi- table become shameless and skilful liars, for tea, sugar, etc. in a grocers' isill if revealed would often amount to nothing but spirits. Once when challenged, a grocer de- fended himself he rtating that it was the custom of the trade to do so. The rev, gentleman also quoted statis- tics relating to the cost to the country and the evils to! intemperance to other directions. He then said to his hearers," Will you ety with the !teeter of Warrington that the drink traffic is not wrong ? " For to say it is not wrong means that it is right. MILL-LANE WESLEYAN MISSION. The annual mission 4ry meting in connection with the above mission was held on Wednesday evening, the chair being occupied be Councillor H. Roberts. The report of the Foreign Missionary Society was given by the Rev. Owen Watkins, and it showed a very encouraging state of things.—Councillor Roberts,in addressing the meeting said that it was a curious fact that there were people who had considerable objection to foreign missions. Seeing that they were commanded by the Great Master Himself to preach the gospel to all the world, he was rather puzzled to account for the opposition which existed. When they remembered the historical fact what the missionaries had done for this country, it was only a matter of common gratitude to do likewise. He found a great many people who objected to foreign missions on the grounds that there was plenty of work to be done at home, and that the money ought not to be wasted upon the foreigners outside. It was a curious fact that those people who raised that objection were not usually distinguished for their efforts in home work. There was a second class ef objectors, who said that the missionaries lived lives of indelence and that they were better off abroad than at home. To those objectors, he had only to mention the names of Moffatt, Livingstone and others. Men who had left their fatherland and families, and who, in HMV cases had lost their lives, or died without a white friend near. Those missionaries who had the good fortune to be married, and had a family, had frequently to send their little ones kome,for years, and did not see them again until they had grown out of recollection. With those facts to be remembered, he could say that the missionaries' lives were not as they were represented by such objectors as he had mentioned. He was glad to be at that mission that night. He believed that they had had notice to quit, but he hoped that they would be able to secure a better and more commodious building before long.—Mr. F. Wright and the Rev. Owen Watkins then addressed the meeting, the latter givine an interesting account of the work in South Africa.—The meeting closed with a vote of thanks to the Chairman, proposed by Mr. W. Brough, and seconded by Mr. W. Light. --

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

A TICIEETLESS TRAVELLER.

At the Warrington Borough Police Court, on Monday morning, George White, dramer, last from Liverpool,was charged with riding on the London and North-Western Railway on November 24th, from Liverpool to Warring- ton without having paid the fare. Prisoner pleaded guilty.—Mr. Fenna' solicitor for the company, prose- cuted, and said that White was found in a third-class compartment at Warrington low level station. When asked for a ticket he said he didn't require one. He had no money and was compelled to travel without a ticket. On his admission he was locked up.—The Chief Con- stable thought that prisoner was there on ticket-of-leave or under police supervision somewhere.—Prisoner after- wards admitted this. He was fined 20s. or 14 days. A SERIES OF TREFTS.—HEAVY PENALTY. Alice Penn and James Taylor, Buttermarket-st., were charged at the Warrington Borough Court, on Monday, with stealing from the back-yard of Joseph Whittaker, on the 16th November, a pair of woollen drawers value 35.; also charged with stealing from 43 Arpley-st., on the 19th November, a white flannel skirt and a petti- coat, the property of Frederick Carlisle; and further with stealing from the shop of John Gibbon, 75 Mersey- at., a man's coat value 2s. 6d. on the 15th November.— Mr. Hunt (chief constable) said the complainant in the first case was Sarah Whittaker who put the drawers to ' bang out on a line about three o'clock on the day named and missed them half-an-hour later. The matter was reported and Detective Taylor from some information went to Mr. Jackson's shop, Church-st., and there found the drawers.—D.S. Monaghan with Inspector Soinks arrested the female prisoner and brought her to the police station. On Saturday night Monaghan appre- hended the male prisoner. On the Monday Frederick Carlis'e had seen the skirt and petticoat safe at his house. These articles were also pledged at Jackson's. With regard to the coat produced it was seen safe at the shop in Mersey-S. on the 15th inst. Monaghan found that the coat had also been pledged by the female nri- soner.—Both prisoners were charged and gave a differ- ent tale. Taylor was sent to gaol for two months' hard labeur in each case making a total of six months, and Penn was sent to gaol for 14 days' hard labour. ASSAULTING a TICICET COLLECTOR. On Monday morning at the borough police court, Warrington, Ralph Dodd, 24, Percy-street. Lovely-lane, was charged with assaulting, at Bank Quay Station, Warringtnn, William Bennett, ticket coll?ctor, whilst in the execution of his duty.—Mr. Fenna, who prosecuted on behalf of the L. and N.-W.R. Co., said that on the night of the 20th of last month between 11.30 and 12 o'clock the defendant with eight others came into Bank Quay station by train from Wigan. On arriving at the ticket harrier the defendant threw his ticket on the fiaor with the intention of diverting the attention of the col- lector from one of his companions, who tried to get past without a ticket. The collector tried to prevent the other man getting away and prisoner obstructed and struck him. The other men jostled Bennett until a cab- driver came to ask them to desist. The men ran away' but the collector follnwed an catching, the prisoner brought him hack. The st,tion_reaster and a constable came to the rescue and took the prisoner to the effice where, in answer to their inqniries, he gave a wrong name and address. His right name was ultimately dis. covered and he was allowed to go. Mr. Fenna asked ; that the prisoner should he taught not to interfere with the officers in their duty.—William Bennett, collector, Bank Quay, said he was struck in the face twice, and further corroborated Mr. Fenna's statement.—William Bell, cab-driver at the Patten Arms hotel, confirmed the last witnesses' statement and s,aid he also was struck. The defendant was not drink. Defendant was fined 40s. and costs or one month's imprisonment (£2 15a. 61. in all).

This sPason's first meetirv, or these old competitors took place on S eurday, at Kendal. The day was gloriously fine,but there was gray a moderate number of spectators, which however is not a very uncommon thing at the Northern town. Warrington had only a moderate team being weakened in the forward rank. Jolley and Dakin were of course absent, their services being revired by their county at Fallowfield, and other absentees were Fair Turner, Thorniley, Nevins and Buckley. Kendal were also .minus a good three-quarter in the person of Langley. In the absence of Turner, Burt.-,n captained the team. The following is the full list of the two op- posing teams : ! Warrington : Boscow, full back; Cunningham, Morris, Eleesom, and Barton, three-quarters ; B ate and ' Clarke, half backs ; Davies, Berry, Taylor, Savage, Bailey, Andrew's, Houghton and Randles. Kendal Hornets : Kitchen, full back ; Braithwaite, Hill, Cole- man, and Holmes, three-quarters; Garnett and Parsons, half backs ; Deason, Major, Nicholson, Favrcett.Caradus. Beard, Wilson and Heap. Referee. Mr. Fawcett, Barrow.

Bandies' kick-off was not well returned, and Warring- ton at once rushed to the quarter flee. Their stay,how- ever, was but of short duration, for the northern forwards smartly dribbled the ball to half way, where play raged for Some trrne. Burton was the first player to become prominent, for accepting a pass from Bate, he ran almost over, but found Braithwaite too good for him. Kitchen now punted to Boecow and that player returning well, Bur- ton was again almost over, Coleman this time staving off the danger. Fine passing was now witnessed be- tween Braithwaite, Hill, Coleman, and Holmes which transferred the scene of operations to the opposite end of the field, but Boscow was all there and would not , allow them to score. A prolonged attack by the Hornets , ended in them being allowed a free kick in a dangerous , position. Taylor relieved Warrington's fears however by charging the ball down, and Cunningham, and Moores took play to half way. Holmes here had a sprint down the field, and just as a score seemed certain, Cun- ningham's great speed enabled him to catch the north- man and promptly floor him just in time. The same player securing a pass from Clark gained great advan- tage by a run and a judicious kick. Coleman now made his mark in a favourable position, but the place not being taken, nothing came of it. Scrambling play fol- lowed, chiefly to Warrington's advantage, and Davies and Bailey almost dribbled the ball over, Holmes just keeping them out. Fleesom was the next to be danger- ous, for intercepting a pass from Holmes, he made a strong run which was brought to an end by Kitchen. Befrton then took the ball to the line, and apneared to have scored, but only a minor was conceded. Half time soon afterwards arrived with a clean sheet. The opening exchanges in the Second half between Caradus and Moores was in favour of the latter, bat the Hornets were the first to be dangerous and Braithwaite forced a minor. The kick out saw the wirepullers' for- wards attacking in a body, their progress being brought to an end at half way by Hill. A grand series of pa.seine between Moores, Heesom, Cunningham and Clarke ended in the latter passing fencer& From the ensuing scrimmage, Braithwaite intercepted Rate's pass, and having a clear field before him he raced away hut was caught by Bailey at the critical moment. Warrington returned to the attack, and Kitchen was compelled to run over his own lines. He was successful in getting his kick, ancl luckily for Kendal, the ball just grazed a Warrington player, thus placing the whole of the players on-side and half way was reached. This was a very leeler let-off for the Hornets indeed. The Hornets rarely had a look in after this, and repeated niches by Warrinatnn were ably checked by the home defenders. Moores looked dangerous het found Kitchen and Holmes tin safe to woe. Burton now had a drop at zeal which just. missed its destination, and another minor was conceded. Some grand work wee done here by Cunningham who had the worst of luck on two occasions when at the point of scoring. As time drew near, Warrington redoubled their efforts to score, and some fine rushes were made by all the threequarters. Bmiten on one occasion, just before the whistle blew, was pulled down on the wrong side when grounding the ball. When no side was called, nothing tangible was 'cored and the game thus ended in a pointless draw,the third which Warrington has played already this season. It roust he confessed that War- rington were not favoured hy fortune, for they held the upper hand the greater portion of the game, especially near the elnee. The ormeeina forwards Were about equal, but the Warrington halves were the superior of the Hornets, who Were too fond of Std. play. The threeemartere also were pretty equally matched, hut there is no comparison between the two full barks. Beseow Seems to play better every match, and can Sat- urday, he certainly excelled himself, for he did net make a single mistake the whole of the afternoon. His fielding and tackling were always sere, and his returns almost invariably found touch. His vie-a-vie is a rent young player being only seventeen years of nr., and it is therefore not to he eirpected that he would equal Posenw. The drop-nut witnes;ed Runcorn pressing, but High am pass- ing smartly to riroady, the latter cleared Brown, and ,1 nut in a nice dodgy run until stopped by Pendlebury. Half-time saw Runcorn leading by a goal. Antrobus re-started the game, Gregory returning with a flying kick—which for once came off—to centre. Broady at this time made a splendid run, dodgins, in and out in capital fashion, and was ultimately "held by Hughes. The cheering had hardly died away when Isherwood took an opponent's: pass, and the hall was eventually taken Main(' the line, Hughes touching down with no time to spare. The game was very even at this period, and some tall punting was seen on both sides. Brown kicked very strongly, and following up at double quick time FPCIIrPti Cross with the ball in his hands, much to the surprise of that player and the amusement of the spectators. The shades of evening were falling fast,anci it was now only possible to see the outlines of the differ- ent players. Flug,hes, however, was visible threading his way in capital style, and although getting past the Stockton Heath halves and three-quarters, he failed to elude Flamblett, who played a goad l game throughout. Score :—Runcorn, one goal ; Stockton Heath, nil.

A DEFENCE OF THE FRENCH NATION.

The annual foreign missionary meeting was held at Bewsey-road Wesleyan Chapel, Warm:4ton, on Monday evening, when there was a fairly good attendance. The chair was occupied by Mr. John Bennett, of Bewsey Hall, and he was supported by the Revs. D. A. de Mouilpied, (French mis,ionarY,) G. Lowe, (Transvaal,) J. Kirtlan,Owen Watkins,and G. D. Mason.

the Rev. G. D. MASON, in reviewing the work in vari- ous parts of the world, said that in the majority or: cases very encouraging reports were received. In France, the influence of the Methodist church was out of all propor- tion to the number of members. Progress was also re- ported in Germany, Austria, and Italy. A great advance was reported in South, and South Central Africa. In Ceylon, professing Christians outnumbered both Mohammedans and Hindoos. Their work, as they would all be aware, had been much hindered in China, and the missionaries there had a strong claim upon their sym- pathies. Mr. Mason then proceeded to give the financial statement which we have published previously in our reports of missionary gatherings. The CHAIRMAN made a very humorous speech in which he expressed his hearty s yrnpathy with mission work. He expressed the conviction that never before was War- rington favoured with having four returned missionaries upon the platform at the same time. He hoped that those present would do their utmost to help on the good , work.

The Rev. D. A. DE MOUILPIED then addressed the nve,ting. lie said that th, work in France was of a I different character from most fields of missionary labour, I France ranked high in the civilised world, and I seemed to be out of the sphere of missionary Re bPlieve 1 that they had never beard of France as a mission field. They had always been taught that the French were their I natural enemies, and that an Englishman was equal to three Frenchmen any day. (Laughter.) He asked whether the fact that the French flag floated side by side with the English flag at Balaclava. Inkermann, Alma, and Sebastopol ought not to extinguish that feel- ing of enmity. (Rear, hear.) France had also the re- putation of being the land of atheists, freethinkers, and materialists. The people were called frivolous French, and the country was that of Voltaire and Zola. But in spite of all the canvassing of M. Zola among the mem- bers of the Ace &my of Immortals, the highest literary society in France,he had been unable to become a mem- ber of that society, and his reputation rested not so much with his countrymen as with the representative Press of England. At the present time there was not an accredited teacher of materialism in Paris. A great deal was said about civil marriages, and the great pro- portion of funerals which took place without the pre- sence of a priest, but for all that, France was the most religious State upon the Continent of Europe. The Frenchmen were determined that no priest should inter- fere in any political elections in'the country. The French were beaten in the last war, said Renan, by the German schoolmaster, and the want of education they owed to the government by the priests. Since that war, they had determined that the priest should have no more power over education, and the education of the young was now under the direction of the State. The charges of persecution arose from the fact that the French people were determined to have no eclesiastical control either in politics or education, as they had suffered from it for over a thousand years. France was a country of wonderful ideas,and she was not afraid to experiment upon herself. She had been striving for the best form of government which would cause the greatest happi- ness to her sons. She had been waiting long for a Saviour; not one after the type of Boulanger, or Napo- leon 1., or Napoleon 111. She wanted a Saviour who would speak to her reason and magnificent intellect, and to her conscience ; a Saviour who would speak to her heart, that large, loving, enthusiastic heart, which was capable of suc,h magnificent outbursts of self- sacrifice. He knew one Saviour who would do this, and that was Jesus of Nazareth. To-day there was no country more open to the preachers. They could go and preach everywhere. Religious liberty was the birth right of every Frenchmaniand he could go to any village and preach the gospel. There was admirable work be- ing done in France, and nowhere were such glorious vic- tories won for Jesus Christ. The work, therefore, was of a most hopeful character, and he was sure that it would continue to be even more hopeful in the future. (A pplause. )

• The Rev. G. LOWE said that it had been his joy to la- bour in two different fields. He was at the West Coast o Africa at the ssron time as the Rev. G. D. Mason, and after returnit,g England for six months he was sent to join the Rev. Owen Watkins, in the Transvaal, where he was engaged in pioneer mission work. In that work they had the most successful results in modern Methodism. Mr. Lowe then gave figures to prove the astonishing progress made in South Central Africa by Christianity, since 1882. He said that the gospel had gained such a hold that it would never be driven out. They had, however, only been successful at the fringe of I the country. There were thousands outside untouched, and for these they required more funds. Much had been done, but to extend their influence they must trust to the people for support. There were numbers of invitations from villages outside their influencr, but missionaries could not be sent owing to that reason. He appealed to everyone present to do his or her utmost in this good cause. Mr. Lowe gave a most interesting account of his work in South Central Africa, and his speech was listened to with great attention.

A coll-ction in aid of foreign missions was then taken, And the meeting closed with a hearty vote of thanks to the Chairman and deputation, which was proposed by the Rev. J. Kirtlan, seconded by Mr. W. Brough and supported by the Rev. Owen Watkins, who informed the audience that Mr. Lowe was returning to his work in Africa in February.

WARRINGTON GYMNASIUM.

GRAND GYMNASTIC DISPLAY.. The Gymnasium, Palmyra Square, was well filled on Saturday evening when this season's first gym- nastic display took place. In addition to the exhi- bitions by the members of the gymna,iurn there was a tug-of-war competition in which seven teams composed of the picked men from different works in the town took part, the winner being "taken on" by the gymnasium team. Mr. T. Morris, F.G.S., officiated as chairman, and at 7.30 announced that the dumb-bell exercise would be gone through by the gym- nasts, in musical time. This exercise and also those of the bar-bells and Indian clubs were gone through with creditable precision. Five members gave an excellent rings exhibition and then the first round in the tog-of-war csnsisting of two heats each team, was fought in which the following teams of six men each eompeted—Wilderspool Brewery (s. Harmer's team) 65st. 61bs. v. Crosfield's Soap Works,B4t. Bnas. ; Wilders- pool Brewery (J. Guest.'.; team) 65st. 13lbs. v. Warring- ton Psstmen, 63st. Blbs. ; Sons of the Gael (Pearson and Knowles) 85t. 911,5. v. Longford Wire Works, 64st. 7lbs. and BPWSEV Forge—a- bye-85st. 211 s. Crosfield's Soap Works won in the first two, Willerspool Brewery easily in the second, and Sons of the Gaol, after a very hard struggle, won in the third. In the . parallel bar exhibition the men engaged were led off by Mr. Kinsley (director) and showed in the skilful manner in which they Went through their performances that Mr. Kinsley's training had been very efficacious. In the seeond round of the tug of war Crosfi9ld's Soap Works beat Guest's Brewery team after a well contested couple of heats and Bowsey Forge were not much pot to it to beat the Sons of the Gael. Mr. W. S. France (gymna- sium) then gave evidenee of his muscular power by the way in which he levelled the 561 b duinh-bells and a hug 120 lb. bar-bell. The performances on the horizonta bar were very well gone through,especially the vaulting, in which J. Podrnore was certainly the best. In the high jumping some clever exploits were witnessed,tint J. Porlmore and I. Bowen out-jumped their brother gymnasts considerably. Much interest was manifested when Crosfleld's Slap Works and the Bewsey Forge team put in their appearance ta " stretch the rope " in they tug of war final. In the first heat, for a few minutes- . neither side seemed to gain the advantage until Cros- field's by a mighty effort gradually drew their opponents over the line. The second heat was not near so exciting, Crosfield's winning easily. Some apprehension was shown by friends of the Gymnasium team when their time came to gtru7ele with the victorious Crosfielders for the laurels. The first heat was won by the Soap Works team but the Gymnasts equalised in the second. This necessitated another he it in which, after a short sharp struggle the trained strength of the gymnasts told and they mused their opponents to reluctantly " toe the line." In the course of the evening, Mr. Morris men- tioned that early in To nary another display would be given, and it would be for the benefit of the conductor, Mr. J. Kinvley, who he was sure. was worthy of all consideration, .(Aoplause.) The following members of the Gymnasium took part in the working out of the programme :--111essrs. J. Podmore. G. N. Vernon, A. EAItLESTOWN WESLEYAN BAND OF HOPE.

The annual festival in connection with this society took place on Saturday evening in the Wesleyan schoolroom, Earlestown. Theproceedings were initiated by a tea, at which about 150 were present. After tea a public meeting and entertain- ment was held in the upper room, over which the Rev. J. Bartley presided, supported by the Rev. J. Burrows, of Hoylake, Miss Burton and Mr. J. Graham. The report for the past year was read by the Secretary (Mr. J. Marsh) as follows:—" We have reached another milestone, as it were, in our journey, and we now stand on the threshold of another year, but before marching forward on our journey we look back on the year that is past, and mark the struggles we have had, and the difficulties with which we have had to contend and note the victory won, and the progress made. In doing so I cannot say that we are altogether satisfied with the result of the year's work, but something has been attempted, and something has been done. We trust some seed sown, may yet spring up and be seen in the days to come. When we consider the influence for good wielded by this society in the past, and how those who have passed through it are working to day for the same objects in many places and var- ious lands,and of others who have passed from their labours but whose work still continues to go on in our midst, we feel encouraged to press on and try to make our teaching more decided and definite,that the fruit of the present sowing may be finer and more plentiful in the future than that of. the past. Our object remains the same 'To save the fallen and to prevent others from falling,' but principally to protect the children from the awful havoc wrought .by drink. To this end the Society was founded and for this we are working. Since our last festival we have held 12 meetings, and enter- tainments, average attendance 182, including a public meeting following up the efforts of Temper- ance Sunday presided over by the Rev. T. R. Matthews, and addressed by Revs. E. R. Davies and J. Cottam. Once more a public enterainment on behalf of our blind brother E. Hughes, has been given by the Society, and as the result .E 5 was handed over to Bro. Hughes. At each meeting some speaker has pressed home the important truths of Temperance. The average attendance at these meetings has been 182. Another step forward has been taken during the year as follows : Arrangements have been made for the holding of a Gospel Temperance service once a month on a Wed- nesday evening. The attendance at these services so far has not been very large. We are looking forward to better things in the future. Also once a quarter' arrangements have been made for a tem- perance acldress in the Sunday School so that seed is therefore dropped and we look for the fruit. As to finances, the year was begun with the sum of 13s. 10. due to the late secretary. We have received during the year from all sources £lO 41.10 d. and spent £lO ss. 7-o.,includino: the balance owing to the late Secretary at the beginning of the year, so that you will see at the present time there is a deficit of 9ii.which will soon be a thing of the past: —(Applause). Addresses were delivered during the evening by the Chairman, Rev. J. Burrows and Miss Burton. The following programme was also sub- mitted in a pleasing manner. Part song " Gaily, gaily," choir; song, "The king's champion," Mr. J. Roberts; recitation, "Save the children," Miss E. Pearson; song," hen the tide comes in," Miss May Clare; piano solo, "Heather Bell," Master B-rtie Evans; recitation, "Take care," Miss J. Pickett ; song, ",The king's own," Mr. F. Clare; action song, "Strike of the beer jugs," choir; recitation, " Tem- perance speech in rhyme." Mr. G. Warburton; duet, "A. B. C.,' Miss May Clare aud Mr. F. Clare; song, "Flight of ages," Miss May Clare; recitation, "Down with the traffic," Master J. Eckerslev; part song, "Whatever you are, be brave, boys," choir: Miss Greenall accompanied in her usual pleasing manner. At the close a vote of thanks was accorded the Chairman and all who had taken part, on the motion of Mr. J. Tl. Graham, seconded by Mr. W. Pearson. Sunday was observed at the Wesleyan Chapel, as Temperance Sunday, when special ser- mons were preached morning and evening by the Rev. J. Burrows, of Hoylake. In the Sunday school an address was given by Q.M. Sergeant Roberts in the morning, and in the afternoon the Rev. J. Burrows presided over a meeting, and addresses were delivered by Mr. E. Weaver and Mr. W. Clare. The collections during the day amounted to .E 3 103.

THE TRANSFER OF THE CHESHIRE PARISHES TO THE RUNCORN

The Cr.Enx announced that the Local Government Board had confirmed the order of the Joint Committee of the Cheshire County Council transfering the Cheshire. parishes of Grapnenhall. Thelwall and Latchford With- out to the Runcorn Union. He said that it was very evident that. there was not sufficient reason for not con- firming the the recommendation of the Joint Committee. He could nnly say that there was a strnm.,, case made out in respect to, 'Latchford Without. They would now have to balance their accounts for the parishes, ,for the present quarter. Mr. Sutton then gave some very interesting figures regarding these ?Parishes. He said that in 1890 the rateable valne of the Lancashire townships of th,, Union was £307.299 and of the Cheshire townships £l6 394 Tn 1894,the Lancashire townships VC'er, valued at £337,851, and the Cheshire tnwnships, £16,412. It would therefore be seen that Lancashire townshipshad increased during the past four years more than double the entire value of tho Cheshire townships. It had also hen!, said that there were no paupers in those townshins, tmt this was the ease in almost all rural townships. He might mention Winwipk, which had a rateable value of more than all the Cheshire townships in question put together yet had not a single .pauper.

STOCKTON HEATFT.

APPLF.TON PARnCITTAL COMMITTEE. —The minutes of this cornnsittee were submitted on Tuesday morning at the meeting: of the Runcorn Rural Sanitary Authority. The committee's meetin, was held at the Bridge Inn, Stockton Heath, on the 220 d ult., under the chairman- ship of Mr. Evres. The Clerk read a letter from Mr. Robert Davies in which he regretted to say that the water supply to his house had been very fitful—for , hours together—and even on Sundays there was'no water on the ground floor. He had paid £BO to bring the water to his house, and had paid the rate ever since. He therefore felt he hid a right to a supply, especially as he had been deprived of it owing to the extensions made by the waterworks of late.—lt was. resolved that a copy of the letter be forwarded to the Town Clerk of Warrington with the request that he would lay it before the Water Flornmittee.—Mr. Farrington, in his report to the committee, asked for the resolution ap- proving of thff plan of a new house proposed to be erected be Mr. Ashton Hayes at Stockton Heath he re- scinded. as he had concluded to erect two instead of one This was acceded to, and permission was granted for him to proceed with the erection of the two houses. should they he to the satisfaction of the Inspector and Mr. C. W. Davenport. An .amendment plan of a new house to be erected by Dr. Parker, at Stockton Fleath, was submitted and approved of. The committee re- commended that certificates of occupation The granted to Messrs. W. Davenport and ;I. Clarke for four houses at Stockton Heath.—The Inspector reported that there had been a stoppage in the sewer in Walton-road, Stockton Heath, and that he had had the same removed. He also reported that Creswell brook, near to the Vicarage at Stockton Heath, was in such a state as to be a nuisance, caused by the sewage from certain hous7,s in Walton In- ferior beimg emptied into it. A committee was appointed to act con-jointly with a similar committee to he ap- pointed by the Sanitary Authority. The minutes of th committee were adopted.

THE LATEST STEAM MAN.

A" steam man" is the latest American novelty. The inventor, Professor George Moore,' of Scotch, English, Irish, and Dutch extraction, according to an American authority,made a similanapparatus with partial success a number ot years ago, but his present venture is larger and intended to draw a waggon. The figure, 61t. high, is encased in metal, and looks like a knight in mediseval armour, with helmet and visor, smoking a cigar. The cigar, however, is an escape pipe, and the plumes on the helmet veil the top of the chimney. The furnace and boiler are contained inside the trunk, and the moving mechanism in the rest of the body. The man walks briskly at a pace of four or five miles an hour.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The fortnightly meeting of this board was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday. Mr. Mclntyre occupied the chair, and there were also present : Rev. J. W. N. Tanner, Dr. Gastaldi, Messrs. J. TH2 TRANSFERENCE OF CHESHIRE PARISHES.

The Local Government Board writing on the 15th ult. stated that they had had under their consideration the report ot the inquiry held regarding the order of the Joint Committee ot the Lancaster and Chester County Councils, providing for the transfer of the townships of Latchford Without, Grappenhall, and: Thelwall, from the Warrington Union ana Rural Sanitary district to the Runcorn Union and Rural Sanitary district. The Board had arrived at the conclusion that no sufficient grounds exist for their refusing to confirm the order in question. The Board would therefore proceed to con- firm the order with such modifications as they might deem necessary for conveying its objects into effect.— The Clerk read the order conferring the transfer.

===== 0002601_18941201_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
THE VACANT SCHOOLMASTERSHIP

The Clerk read letters from Mr. Andrew Nord, of the Liverpool Industrial Schools, applying for the vacant situation of schoolmaster, at the Union schools, and also from Mr. Harry Allison, last from the North Lancashire Reformatory, Garstang. The two candidates were called into the room and ultimately the beard decided to appoint Mr. Allison. AN INCREASE OF SALARY.

In accordance with notice of motion Mr. Heywood proposed that Mr. de Prez's salary be increased from £75 a year to £lOO. He said the committee had unani- mously decided to grant the increase of salary, and he also alluced to Mr. de Prtz's extensive district.—Mr. Owen sec,inded the motion.—Mr. Pritchard, in moving an amendment that the matter be adjourned until the new district council be formed, stated that it was not a time for raising salaries.—Mr. Horton seconded the amendment.—A long discussion took place in which Messrs. Lea, Stokes, Jones, Brandreth and Heywood took part, and it was ultimately decided by 13 votes to 12 to grant the increase of salary.

===== 0002601_18941201_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
APPOINTING AN ASSESSOR.

Mr. Knowles proposed, on the recommendation of the Assessment Committee, that Mr. Owen, surveyor, of Warrington, be appointed to value the works of Messrs. Ebenevr Timmins and Sons, Limited, and of the Aus- tralian Alum Company, Limited, both of Runcorn, for the purp)se of the assessment thereof.—Mr. Jones wished to know who did similar work before.—The Chairman said the late Mr. Linaker.—On being asked as to whether the committee were unanimous in their recommendation, the Chairman said that two names were before the committee, but they decided upon Mr. Owen. The committee's decision was confirmed. There was no other business of public interest. THE ATTENDANCE OF MEMBERS

RUNCORN RURAL SANITARY

AUTHORITY.

A meeting of this authority was held in Dutton Workhouse on 'Tuesday. Mr. Mclntyre occupied the chair and there were also present: Rev. J.W.N. Tanner, Messrs. J. Knowles, C. T. Whitely, S. Wood- ward, J. Weir, T. Lewis, E. Aaron, J. Stoke, W. Ellison, H. E. Heywood, J. Brandreth, C. W. Daven- port,J.Pickering, W. Turner, J. Baguley, E. Brather- ton, J. Priestner, S. Horton, A. Broome, J. Ashton •(clerk) G. Ashton (ftssistant clerk), J. Farrington (nuisance inspector.) THE MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT.

Dr. Adams submitted a report stating that, in accord- ance with their tf?:irection, he had again inspected in company. with the Halton Parochial Committee, the Halton Brook, and the sewerage scheme which drained into it, and was of the opinion that an extension of the existiug system by the addition of another intercepting tank, would meet the requirements of the Pullution of Rivers (Prevention) Act, such addition to be accom- panied ith proper provision for emptying the tanks, and efficient supervision by a responsible person. There was only one fresh case of scarlet fever at Kingsley since the closing af the schools. One fresh case of scarlet fever had occurred at Norley. No fresh cases of the tame ciisf ase had occurred at Weston or Weston Point. The report was adopted.

===== 0002601_18941201_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
ISOLATION HOSPITALS.

Mr. Potts wrote, stating that hie Public Health Com- mittee had requested the county medical officer of health to report as to the existing isolation hospitals; and as they conEldered that no delay should take place in put- ting the Act into operation they had instructed him to bring tbe matter under the notice cf the various rural and urban sanitary authorities in the county with a request that they will take the Act into their early con- sideration, and be prepared to consult with his Commit- tQe early in the coming year as to the hospitals which should be established to supply the wants of the county. He would be obliged by the Clerk submitting the corn munication by his Authority at an early date.

===== 0002601_18941201_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SMALL-PDX HOSPITAL AGAIN.

Mr. Heyviood moved that the smallpox hospital at Moore be taken for the further period of one year frorr the 31st December next at a rent at the rate of £2O per annum, subject to the tenancy being determined at an earlier date in the event of the Ship Canal Company re- quiring the land, by their giving to the Authority one month's notice in writing to determine the same. In doing so Mr. Heywood read letters to the effect that the statement previously reported that Mr. Evans,the agent for Sir R. Brookes, had offered £2O per annum for the hospital was not correct.—Mr. Aaron seconded the motion.—Mr. Brand reth was of the opinion that the authority should not pay the amount named, stating that is sas owing to the Ship Canal Company that small- pox was brought into the district. He moved that a deputation.consisting of the Clerk, the Chairman, and Mr. Brandreth, and Mr. Williams, wait upon the Ship Canal Company and lay the whole matter before them. The amendment on being put to the meeting was carried only the proposer and seconder of the original motion voting against it.

WILKINSON'S SPECIAL ELIX

THE SOLICITOR- GENERAL ON

BETTING.

• At the annual dinner of the Gimcrack Club. at York, on Tuesday night, the Soliciter-General (Mr. Frank Lockwood, Q.C.), proposed the toast " The stewards and members of the Jockey Club." He said it was legal in this country to make a bet. An Act had been passed declaring that it was legal under certain conditions to make bets. There was acme doubt as to the conditions under which betting was illegal ; but when that doubt had been cleared up he was confident that no body of men would be more anxious to uphold the law than the stewards of the Jockey Club. There was a widespread mama for gambling, and it had a bad effect on sport,but he protested against racing, our national E port, being told responsible for all the evils attendant upon gam- bling. It was as unreasonable to hold racing responsible for all the evils of gambling as to • hold the stock ex- chance responsible for the system of bucket shops.

ROMAN- F [TES.

At the fete of 100 days given by Titus in the year 80, 5,000 swag, beasts of various kinds were shown in one ddy, and at the spectacle given by Trajan, which lasted four months, no less than 11,000 animals were exhibited. Never (says the "Cornhill Magizine") was there such a splendid opportunity for the naturalist and the anatom- ist. The imperial menageries were, as might he expected, extraordinarily rich in rare creatures, and would quite have thrown our modern Zoological Gardens into the shade. The cost of the keep of so many carnivorous animals was enormous, and Caligula, in time of dearth, seriously considered Use advisability of using criminals as fodder for his savage pets. The necessities of trans- port gave a wonderful stimulus to certain trades. The smiths and carpenters were kept hard at work making strong cages, while innumerable traps and lassoes had to be provided for the hunters who were employed in all parts of the world to take ferocious beasts alive. In spite of anxious care and vast expense, many were the dis- appointments caused by the non•arrival of eagerly expected living cargoes, owing to storms and contrary winds. During the long voyages and the interminable journeys overland, when the heavy cages were dragged by slow bullocks, many of the animals died, and others arrived in so pitiable a plight that they were quite in- capable of "showing fight." The animals were usually introduced into the arena ornamented with variegated scarves, metal plaques, gold leal,and tinsel. They were Ills) painted in gaudy colours. Bulls were painted white, sheep purple, Dstriches vermilion, and the lions had their manes gilded. The creatures were excited to fight by means of whips and darts, and sometimes two of different species were tied together until,frantic with rage, the tore each other to pieces. Curious and un- natural contests were arranged, buffaloes being set to :lent with bears, lions with giraffes, arid tigers with rhinocers,ses.Chasseurs, with English or Scotch hounds, fc tight the most savage beasts, and a distinguished champion eculd kll a bear with a blow of his fist, or vanquish a lion after blinding him by throwing a cloth over his eyes. Die 11,eman anniial trainers must have been men of extraordinary genius, and would certainly 1,,,ve put our modern trainers to the blush. We hear of Julius Cie-as being lighted to his house by elephants carrying torches in their trunks,and Mark Antony being drawn through the streets by lions harnessed to his chariot. Unwieldy bulls learned to walk on their. hind legs ; s,aes were trained to obey the bit, and panthers to bear the yoke. Peaceahle antelopes were taught to figl7t, with their horn7.l ad -fierce dogs were rendered as docile as dogs.
