Letters to the Crittor.

TEE WARRINGTON I.L.P. AND THE RECENT DEBATE ON THE HOUSE OF LORDS. Sir,—l am instructed by the executive of the above party to state that Mr. A. Brouchemite was not author- ised to represent the Independent Labour Party at the recent debate on the House of Lords at the Masonic Room, Bold-street, nor are the views expressed by him on that occasion endorsed oy the party.—Yours J. PODMORE, jun., Hon. Sec.

"TILE OBSERVED OF ALL OBSERVERS." Sir,—There are one or two things I want to ask you about. I saw,a local advertisement the other day about " consecrated manure," and I wondered whether it would be worth my while to buy some or not. You see, I'm very fond of gardeeieg, but so far I've been content with old-fashioned flowers, such as primroses, wall- flowers, lupins, pansies, and such like, but if you think "consecrated manure" would help me La raise holy lowers, then I'll try it. They would be so useful for Christmas decorations, wouldn't they? But I can't quite make out where it is to be bought. It says "Borough of Warrington" on the top of the ad vertise- ment, and so I suppose that means that the Corporation have something to do with it, but I don't think it will be any use writing to them about it, because they never " consecrate " anything. and I don't think anything "consecrated" emanates from Academy-Bt. either. Perhaps it is all excreted there—l mean secreted. How stupid of me! I see the agent's name is given, and so I'll write to him about it, because he says we 'may rely upon it that he will see they are supplied with a good and reliable manure." How accommodating he is! I have no doubt that if I trust to "God and economical government," and plenty of "consecrated manure."my garden will surprise somebody before long. —Yours truly,

SWEET WILLIAM.

===== 0002601_18941201_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
FOREIGN COMPETITION IN THE IRON TRADE.

Sir,—!' your issue of 17th, Mr. Monks states they have made many inquiries and have acquired a good deal of information, and adds that there is not the slightest intention on the part of employers to ask for a reduction of the present wages basis. Be further says that royalty does not affect the present wages of the bar iron trade, that Warrington is at no disadvantage as regards railway rates, and that English manufacturers are at no disadvantage comparea with Continental manufacturers in the cost of raw material. Will Mr. Monks kindly explain for the benefit of the public where the disadvantage is P If a royalty of over ss. per ton on the pig used in Warrington for bar iron does not affect the manufacturer, perhaps Mr. Monks will show why. If it is not raw material or railway freights and royalty what is it? Not wages we are told. As one of the gentlemen who made the inquiry tells us, that in sted Belgium has onls. a slight advantage, while in iron wages are individually about 30 per cent. lower, and it takes three men to do the same amount of work as two of ours, so that, taking cost per ton, English is lower than Belgian. Is the cause, as one of the inquirers states, that Uri Belgians have made a speciality of one or two branches, and that we in England had nothing equal to the plant that had been put down and was still being put down in Belgium. And, as another of the inquirers states, they beat us in the completeness and high standard of their outfits which are always up to date. They are not, Like many works in this country, overladen with inferior or obsolete machinery. Trusting you will insert this and Mr. Monks will give the public his explanation, I am, yours Sze.,

ONE INTERESTED.

Sir.—Your correspondent, Mr. T. Malkin, commences his letter by asking three questions to which I will reply reverting the order in which they are asked. No. 3. There does not seem to me to be any need to introduce South Wales, since no such comparison is possible, the iron trade of that district being no longer in existence. No. 2. The amount of coal raised per man per annum may be as Mr. Malkin states, but is it worth discussing the point when the coal is delivered to the Belgian iron works at the same rate per ton at which suitable coal an be bought in Lancashire? No. 1. As regards the Belgian iron-worker. I believe he is quite equal to the present Englishman,hut whether it takes three or more Belgians to do the same work as is done by two Englishmen is of no importance when the coat of labeur is worked out per ton of iron made. • I have the wages paid at a works in Belgium ; one that has been most successful in taking the orders which formerly came to this country. The total paid is as near as possible one half the amount paid in Warrington, or, to be quite accurate, the amount paid here is 7s. per ton more than is paid by the Belgian manufacturer, even after allowing for the reduction of 21 per cent, in this district, which takes effect from Monday last. This is a fair comparsion, the class of work done, the size of the rolls, range of sizes manufactured, and materials used being exactly the same in both countries. How this difference is to be met without relucing wages still further is the problem yet to be solved, but it is one in which the workman has a greater interest than the manufacturer and the sooner he begins to re- cognise the gravity of the situation the better it will be Ai' *4- - Id o 041.: to show the enormous growth of this Belgian il3petitionL during the last few years, I give the im- ports by British India of the following sections of iron— barangle, bolt_ and rod iron, hoops, sheets and plates, beams, pillars, girders, and bridge work, all of which are manufactured in Warrington : Prim From From England. Belgium. other c'tries. Total. Tons. Tons. Tons. Tons. 1888-89 125.851 19,527 223 135,601 1889-90 89,907 28.543 204 118,654 1899 91 99,088 28.92 347 12F,397 1891-92 83,953 34,980 869 119 802 1892-93 54,539 54,119 986 109.644 The exact figures for 1893-94 are not yet obtainable, but I venture to predict they will show a further falling from England. and a gain to our foreign competitor. In bar iron especially will this be the case, for during the first six months of the current year the quantity im- ported from the United Kingdom is only 22 per cent., as compared with 93 per cent, during the same months of 1889. Now as regards machinery and plant, the Belgian is no better off in this respect than makers in Warrington, ' and his mills do not give a grEater output for the same

class of work. As to quality no one pretends that Bel- gian is equal to Lancashire iron, but the toreign buyer says plainly Belgian quality, is good enough for his pur- pose, and its li,wer price is an additional inducement. Be does not prefer Belgian iron to an English iron when quality and price are equal. So long as this country was the only source of supply, and we had practically a monopoly of the markets of the world, the fkireign buyer had no option but to take what he could get, but now that he can get what he wants, he exercises his right to buy where it is made. We must either supply him on the same terms as our foreign competitors, or be content to lose the trade altogether. As to royalties, I have already said those do not affect the particular matter under discussion, and I fail to see how the difficulty can be met by the introduction of what are purely abstract questions.--Yours truly, F. MONKS.

REFERENCE AT ST. ALBAN'S CHURCR.

Speaking on Sunday morning at the eleven o'clock service in the St. Alban's church, where the deceased gentleman was a constant worshipper, the Rev. Father A. D. Fitch, referrcd to the late Mr. Voisey as follows : I cannot help recording the sense of the loss we have sustained in the death of Mr. Toisey. Warrington has lost an able, energetic, and honoured townsman. His worth was well known to his fellows, all classes and creeds who before now have united in offering him the highest municipal offices. The Catholic body has lost one who was an honour to the community to which he belonged. St. Alban's Church has lost a warns sapporter, and the clergy have lost an amicable personal friend. But the members of his family, who have been bereft of him who was all in all to them, have been stricken by the most sudden and heavy blow. In offering them our sincere sympathy we pray that they Ilkley recognize in this sore trial the hand of Him who gave and who has taken away. We pray that abundant consolation may descend from Heaven into their hearts to enable them to bear their less with patience and resignation. May he rest in peace.

===== 0002601_18941201_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
MAGISTERIAL REFERENCES.

Before proceeding with the business of the Borough Court on Monday morning, the Mayor said he 'would like to say a few words about the loss the Court had sustained in the death of Mr. Voisey. Re announced the deep regret they felt at their loss, and that the Bench desire to express publicly their deep sympathy with Mr. Voisey's family in their bereavement. Mr. White (clerk to the magistrates) endorsed what the Mayor bad said, and added that Mr. Voisey was always regular in his attendance at Court, and in his judicial ceurse he was always just though severe,when necessarily so. Mr. Greenall also agreed with the Mayor, and cave it as his experience of Mr. Voisey that he had always shown the greatest possible kindness, and expressed It as his opinion that the town had suffered a great loss.

Mr. A. Browns, solicitor,said that in the death of Mr Voisey he had lost a personal Mewl, and one to whom he could look with respect. Mr. Voisey was, he con- tended,a man whose judgments one could not object to. He was fair, and sometimes severs. Alderman Harrison said he could hardly resist the temptation to say that in Mr. Voisey the town had sus- tained an irreparable loss. He would go further and say that in whatever capacity they found him he was always the same—in official, private.and business capa- cities. He had nuver heard of Mr. Voisey other than with a feeling of respect for him.%They felt deeply with the household which had been made doolate by the cruel hand of death.

THE FUNERAL

took place at the Warrington Cemetery on Wednesday morning at 12 o'clock. Mns LONGSHORE Porrs , M.D.—Mrs. Lon gsh ore Potts M.D., since leaving Liverpool in 1886 has made a lectur- ing tour throughout the United States and has travelled through New Seal nd and Australia and India. She has now returned to Liverpool and proposes to make her reappearance at the Royal Court Theatre in a lecture to women only, on Tuesday, December 7th, when she will deliver a free lecture to women only. The great furore she created among fashionable and literary circles will not soon be forgotten and great honours have been con- ferred upon her in her native land since she was last here :—The wife of the President, with other ladies of distinction, bestowing upon her the warmest commenda- tions, the somewhat gruesome paraphernalia which was used for illustrative purposes on the previous visit consisting of skeletons, ribs, paintings and things,which gave the petite Quakeress a somewhat mortuary sur- rounding, have been replaced by stereoptican views, consisting of many of art and scenery. It will astonish the women of Liverpool to learn how views of scenery and art can be properly adapted to the discussion of medical and physiological matters,yet both the American papers and those of Landon,pronounce these illustrations as not only applicable but declare them to be among the most beautiful in the world. It is said that she will use upwards of 200 views at each lecture three lectures to ladies will follow on the afternoons of the sth, 6th and 7th of December.

IN PRAISE OF VEGETARIANISM.

At the meeting ot the Baptist Mutual Improve- ment Society held in the Schoolroom, Golborne-et.. on Friday night, there was a good attendance The chairman was dccupied by Mr. N. Balmfoid, Mr. John S. Green entertained the Soci- ety with an address on " Vegetarianism : what it is, and what it will do." After pointing out that abstinence from flesh eating was no new idea, as it is already practised by three-fourths of the peoples of the world, he referred to the generally accepted fallacy as to Vegetarianism, and stated that Vegetarianism was entire abstinence from fish, flesh and fowl, and the substitution of a simpler mode of living than that which prevailed amangst most English people. This was to be found amongst fruits, cereals, pulses, &c., many of which were perfect foods, and all of which contained more real nutriment than could be found in flesh meats, which consisted mostly of water. He contended that man by nature. by instinct, and by moral aspiration was frugivorous ; and that, by departing from his natural mode of feed- ing, he had entailed upon himself much needless suffer- ing. Such diseases as consumption, cancer, trichina, gout, rheumatism were said to be due to indulgence in animal flesh, which was invariably diseased. Instead of living to a good old age, man by his perversity of living. had considerably shortened his life, and rendered himself liable to sudden death. His temper and skin were also irritated and disfigured by his indulgence in flesh meats. As to what Vegetarianism would do, Mr. Green remarked that it would deliver people from pain, it would make life what iv ought to be, hut seldom was— a pleasure, and for women it would greatly lighten their domestic duties. The practice of Vegetarianism would help mist materially to solve the problem of the day, that of the unemployed as the land thrown out of cul- tivation for the production of beef could be used to better advantage. Twelve acres of land devoted to feed- ing cattle gave employment to two men and produced food for one. Under grain, the same land would give employment to 4 men and produce food for 20 persons, whilst under fruit it would employ six men and feed 20. Above all, Vegetarianism would rid us of our national curse that of drunkenness. By discontinuing the use of condiments and stimulating, thirst-producing flesh meats, and substituting thereof the fruits of the earth, men would find their thirsty propensities entirely dis- appear and life assume a new aspect. To his (the speaker's) view, temperance workers were labouring on wrong lines, and until they gave more attention to this question of diet, and its bearing upon the drinking habits of the people, they need not expect any great measure of success. In conclusion, the speaker urged his hearers to give this question of diet reform their serious consideration, and appealed to them to lead a higher and nobler life by freeing themselves from modes of existence more suited to barbaric tribes than civilised beings.—A lively discussion followed, and the speaker having replied, the proceedings closed with votes of thanks.

SOME LABOUR STATISTICS.

The Labour Department of the Board of Trade has begun to issue an annual resume of statistics, which is equally interesting and useful. As bearing on the eight hours question it supplements figures published during the debate on the Mines (Eight Hours) Bill, which showed that as between different districts the hours from bank to bank and the hours worked at the face vary very largely. Here we are told how many mos, the kingdom throueh, work given hours. There were in the coal mines of the United Kingdom in 1890 272.462 getters, 178,148 other workers underground ,and 98,257 surface workers. The proposed limitation to eight hours a day "from bank to bank" would, it appears, make a very considerable change in the total I hours worked. Only 20 4 per cent. of getters put in so little time as this. Of the total number 0 2 per cent. are in the mines 8 hours and under 81 daily. 3 5 per cent. from 81 to 8± hours, 5.7 per cent. from 81, 18'5 per cent. from 81 to 9, 1.9 per cent. from 9 to 91, 292 per cent. from 91 to 9±, and 4 7 per cent. 91 or more. It will be noted that 51.8 per cent. of getters work more than 9 hours a day and over 70 per cent. mere than 81 hours. At the face of the coal, however, exactly 80 per cent. already work eight hours a day or under, and more than 60 per cent. work only 71 hours or under. The agitation for an eight hours hank to bank system involves, therefore, an effort to make employers pay for the time taken up in travelling to and from the place. It is the corollary of the attempt, by enforcing uniform rates of pay throughout the kingdom, to make employers bear all the inequalities of cost in working which differences between one mine and another involve. There is a greater difference between the hours of getters and those of other workers than is generally known. Surface workers are almost all employed longer than 8± hours, and only 13.1 per cent. of such underground workers as are not getters have a shorter day than that. Indeed 66 in every hundred of surface workers and 63 per cent, of the other class are employed for nine hours a day or over; while 13.3 per cent, of these latter work ten hours and more. The hours of surface workers, moreover, are exclusive of meal-times. The history of trade disputes in recent rears show, two marked tendencies. From 1889 to 1893 inclusives the number of disputes reported to the Department, fell steadily from 1,211 to 643 a year ; but the numher of persons affected by such disputes increased from 359,897 in the first year to 570,060 in the last. This appears to fit in with the fact that the number of successful dis- putes in the same period fell from 43 to 35.6 in a hun- dred. Nearly half the trade disputes of England and Wales occur in Yorkshire, Lancashire, and Cheshire. though the population of these three counties is hut 7,884,766 out of 29,002,525. Lancashire and Cheshire, with a population but little greater than that of the Metropolis, had 106 disputes last year against 29 there, while Yorkshire, which comi,ares fairly in point of population with the West Midlands, had 100 disputes against 35 in that group of six counties.

THE EXAMINER, SATURI)AY, DECEMBER 1, 1894. NOTES ON GAMES AND DEATH OF MR. LEWIS VOISEY, ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL. The Warrington team reached the furthest point north, which they have had to go this season,on Saturday,when they met the Hornets who live in Kendal. They were favoured with good weather for which they were very thankful, and they also had a good time in everything but scoring. The Hornets have heen met for a great. number of years now, and the games though in the majority of cases in favour of Warrington, had always been very close and exciting. The team is practically the Westmoreland team, for almost every player who has been chosen to represent Westmoreland, is or has been a member of the Kendal Hornets club. Could all the truant players from Kendal be gathered home again, what a powerful, team could be put telether. But Kendal does not appear to have sufficient attractions to keep her players, and the result is that they are to be found all over the country. The team at the present time is consequently not so strong as it might be.

The game on Saturday was a fairly equally fought game, and the result, a pointless draw, is not very far wrong. Each side had very hard lines at times, though Warrington had slightly the hardeit. The Warring- tonians of course were very much weakened through Vac absence of such players as Turner, Jolley,Dakin Nevins, and Thorniley, and although the substitutes worked with a will, they are not to be expected to ha able to play with the same skill as the veterans.

The Warrington team in consequence of the absence of hotk captain and sub-captain, had to look out for a new skipper, and this they found in the person of Burton, who thus has not had to wait long before attaining to this positien. He can congratulate himself that War- rington did not stiffer defeat under his captainship, if they did not achieve victory.

I will now say a word about the players individually. Boscow comes in for first mention, and he appears to improve every match he plays. He was simply unpass- able or' Saturday. No matter wherever the ball went there was Boscow blocking the way. In fact this was noticed by the spectators, and one of them said in an aggrieved tone, "That bloomin' full back is always in the way of our men." The three-quarter line is now very speedy, and all that is now required is a little better combination. They lacked this somewhat on Saturday, or something more substantial might have been the result. In speed, they were superior to the opposing quartette. The halves played a very good game, and would have played better still, but for the off-side play of the Hornets halves.

The forwards were very equally matched, and it was here that such weighty men as Thorniley and Turner were missed. An old war horse was taken on again in the forwards. This was " Tony " Davies, .who had not played previously for two years. He played with any amount of" dust" nevertheless.and would not disgrace any team.

Today, Leigh commence operations again after their long holiday. They ought to be in good condition by this time, and Warrington being their first opponents will be the first to test them. The match between War- rington and Leigh takes place at the latter town, and to encounter them thevisitors will be represented by the following players :—Boscow. full back ; Burton, Hee- sem, Moore', and Cnnningharn, three-quarters ; Bate and Clark, half backs ; Turner, Jolley, Dalcin, Bailey, Taylor, Berry, Savage, and Andrews, forwards.

What is the matter with the" A " team ? They are not keeping up the form which they commenced the sea- son with. The last match they engaged in previous to Saturday.was with Swinton, when they had to submit to defeat. They had to do likewise again on Saturday when for the third time in succession they were beaten by the Widnes A team. This is a bad record, and our junior champions ought t 3 see that it is altered as soon as possible.

The West End junior teams had their Derby day on Saturday. I allude to the meeting of St. Mary's and the Rangers. Although the match was played on the ground of the Rangers, the " Marys " won comfortably, thus keeping their, unbroken record still untarnished. The Rangers were the first to scnre, but they could not keep it up, and soon lost their advantage.

The Stockton Heath team !suffered their second defeat of the present rpason on Saturday. They tackled the Runcorn " A " at Runcorn, without the assistance of Barber. and the result was that they were beaten by a coal to nil. This is not a thing to be ashamed about, for Runcorn takes seme libeating. They are one of the best " A" teams there are in this part of the country at the present day.

The match in which Lancashire and Yorkshire football enthusiasts take the greatest interest in, is over, and the result is that the Sykes are as jubilant, as the Lanca- shire men are downcast. Lancashire have the finest back combination they have had for some years. but they do not appear to lave played quite up to their form It is suggested that they had not sufficient confidence in Foulkes, the full back, who was playing a very poor gams, and thus were rather disorganised. It was not in the back division in which Lancashire showed the worst however, but amongst forwards. The Tykes, had n tremendous weight in this divison, and the compara- tively light Lancashire forwards were thus heavily handicapped. The only division in which Lancashire excelled was the half back, and it was rather too bad that Varley should have been passed over in the selec- tion of the Northern team. No one expected that York- hire would win by four goals three tries to two goals, and the White Rose has every reason to be proud of its glorious victory.

We regret to announce the death of Mr. Lewis

Voisey,J.P., F.C.A, which occurred in the early hours of Sunday morning, at his residence, 3, Win- marleigh-street. The deceased gentleman,who was 64 years of age, has resided in Warrington for 40 years, and has carried on business as an accountant

for ten years. lie has also taken an active interest in public affairs having been a member of the Town Council, and also a county Councillor for Warring- ton. He was also a magistrate for the borough of Warrington. He first entered the Town Council in 1888, when he was elected for the S.W. ward, sub- sequently, at the revision of the ward, sitting for St. Austin's ward. He retired from the Town Council three years ago. As a Town Councillor, he proved him- self a very useful member, more especially in financial matters, his opinion in affairs of this kind being always accepted with the utmost confidence. On the resigna- tion of Mr. John White he was elected a member of the Lancashire County Council for the borough of Warring- ton, the portion he represented being co-extensive with the Howley, St. Austin's and Latchford Wards, the other local representatives at the time being Mr. F. A. Roberts, for the Town Hall, Whitecross, and Rewsey wards; and Mr. Frederick Monks, for the Orford, St. John's, and Fairfield wards. In the early part of this year, however, he resigned his position owing to increas- ing business responsibilities and impaired health, and was succeeded by Mr. Alderman Francomb, J.P., no contest for the vacant seat taking place. He was raised to the magisterial bench in 1889 and his decisions were always treated with great respect. Some eight days ago he was in the full enjoyment of health to a casual observer. He left his house to take a walk on the afternoon of Saturday week, epparently in this state of health ,but when he returned h w 1,; to be ailing somewhat. His condition rapidly became worse, and he was attended by Dr. T. Starkey Smith and his son, Dr. C. B. Voisey, M.D., F.R.C.S. These two gentlemen subsequently deemed it advisable to hold a consultation with Dr. Drechfield of Manchester, and Dr. Mitchell Banks of Liverpool. On Thursday after- noon an improvement took place in the sufferer's condi- tion, and it was maintained until Friday evening. The following day he had a relapse, and on the Sunday morning he died as stated above. His loss will be , widely felt in Warrington and district, as he was ever ready to help forward any movement which had for its object the good of those by whom he was surrounded. Although a staunch Conservative, Mr. Voisev was very rarely to the fore on political platforms. He was a Roman Catholic, and was a warm supporter of the local Roman Catholic churches. He leaves a widow, and two sons, and twe daughters.

" SMILE 3 OF NATURE."

The artistic decoration of a home with paintings and sculpturf, is an expensive luxury that all cannot afford, hut every home may be made beautiful by nature's deco- rations—a garden—God first planted a garden, "the purest of human pleasures." It smiles upon every in- telligent effort man puts forth, showing how closely nature is allied to man. Be the home ever so humble, it can be cheered by a few bright ever-changing flowers, and in no country is this fact better appreciated than in England. Ingenuity in the arrangement of garden and flower-beds is manifest and may be admired on every hand. The writer knows one old gentleman, whose hair is bleached, and long flowing beard whitened by many winter blasts and summer suns, who in his love for flowers, combines with it his gratitude for life and its blessings by artistically arranging a border for his flower-bed with inverted amber bottles which previously contained Warner's Safe Cure. He says,4` when I look ' at my flowers I am thankful to their creator, and when I look at these empty bottles I am grateful, being re- minded that through their former contents I was spared to enjoy the blessings of life and nature. From childhood the ocean has been my home, rising , from cabin boy to captain. I have visited nearly every part of the globe. For over twenty years I was the picture of health; always robust and hearty. But when trouble came i': attacked me in the shape of sharp, acute, knife-like pains in the right side. These at times were so severe that I would involuntarily fall on the deck, and was obliged to be carried below. At last I was compelled to leave my ship. The doctors said I had muscular rheumatism, but did me no good. I gradually grew worse. At this time my condition was simply terrible,and only those who saw me during those dark hours and have seen nse since my restoration to health, can form any idea of the shadow of death through which I passed. My back and shoulders ached until I imagined myself spread upon the rack, my feet and legs were swollen so that the skin cracked open. My nervous system seemed shattered, my manhood entirely destroyed, and my reason almost dethroned. I was taken to the hospital. They diagnosed gravel in the kidneys, and said I might last a month, but that there was no hope for recovery. My friends gave me up, and as for myself my daily prayer was that God in His infinite mercy would compassionately put an end to my sufferings. Such was my condition when my son brought me a bottle of Warner's Safe Cure. I took it faithfully. In ten days I was better ; in a month able to walk same distance, and in a few months my health was completely restored. This was six years ago, and with the exception of severe attack of Use prevalent influenzs I have enjoyed good health ever since that time." Such was the story of Captain James Paige,of 4, Eddy- stone-terrace, Plymouth, and lie tells us to print this in order that possible sufferers may know how to help themselves in time of need.

The Right Rev. Dr. Whiteside, D.D., Catholic Bishop of Liverpool, attended the St. Alban's and St. Mary's Roman Catholic Churches, Warrington, on Sunday, and conducted services of confirmation. Both churches vc, re beautifully adorned with flowers, and the services were attended by very large congregations. At St. Alban's 278 persons were confirmed, whilst at St. Mary's the number was considerably over 300. His lordship expressed himself highly gratified with the reception he had received from the congregations, and'remarked that it ever was and ever would be to him a pleasure to visit the churches in Warrington. CHILD BURNT TO DRITH.

Mr. S. Brighouse, county coroner, held an inquest on Tuesday on the body of William Henry Lloyd, aged five years, son of Henry Lloyd, 29, Green-st., who died on Saturday evening, from severe burns he received the same morning.—Henry Lloyd said that an elder son had gone downstairs early on Saturday morning and lighted the fire, afterwards going out of the house. Shortly afterwards, whilst witness was dressing, the deceased went downstairs in his night dress. A few minutes later, he heard the child screaming, and rushing downstairs, he found him in flames. Witness seized the boy and , wrapped a large coat around him and thus extin- guished the flames. Some oil was applied to the I burned parts, and Dr. Brookes was called in, but deceased died the same night. Ne one was in the kitchen at the time the accident occurred.—The

VOLIINTHEIt BALL.

There were about 150 couples present at the Drill Hell on Monday evening, on the occasion of the non-commissioned officers ball. Dancing commenced at eight o'clock to music supplied by the band of the corps, under the direction of Bandmaster Armstrong, and was kept up with spirit until nearly three o'clock on the following morning. Bugle- Major Piiui officiated as M.C. The room had been very tastefully decorated for the occasion under the superintendence of Sergeant Joseph Hesketh. The refreshments were supplied by Mr. H. Gittens, of the Baths Hotel, Sankey-street. The company included Major Ridgway and most of the other officers, and the staff-sergeants and sergeants from the barracks at Orford had been invited to attend and they did so in large numbers. The Bal Committee consisted of the sergeants of the corps with Colour-Sergeant Spencer as president, Colour Sergeant Potter as treasurer, and Colour-Sergeant jtennion as secretary. The proceedings passed off in a very successful and enjoyable manner.

ALL HOUSEHOLD LINEN hemmed and marked, free of charge.—J. and W. Durrozr, Sankey-et.. Warrington,—Advt. SUNDAY AFTERNOON SERVICES.

A large congregation assembled at Bold-st. Wesleyan Chapel on Sunday afternoon at the usual people's service. The first item on the programme was a minuet from "Samson," by the orchestral band. An oboe duet, "I waited for the Lord," was well rendered by Messrs. Davies and Ward. Mr. Waring, who occupied the chair, said he was glad to see such a large congregation. He believed there were those present who were not in the habit of attending the house of God, and he knew there was one among them who had not been in a place of worship for twenty years. Mr. Summersgill was then called upon to give an address. The true man, he said, was not measured by inches, by weight, or appearance. The most crippled and deformed were often God's best children. Let him exhort all of them to take Christ as their example, and to endea- vour to follow in His footsteps. "The promise of , life," was then effectively sung by Miss Gunton, and the orchestral band also played, "Gloria." A most hearty and bright service was brought to a termination by the pronouncing of the benediction.

BRAVE CONDUCT OF A GENTLEMAN.

A number of boys were playing in a small boat just below Paddington locks on Sunday afternoon, when one of their number, a boy named George Hough, aged about 14 years, son of Alfred Hougb,of Howley-laue, Warrington, joiner, by some means fell or was pushed into thEt.water. He was unable to swim, and it soon became evident that he would be drowned unless assistance was immediately forthcoming. The boys in the boat were too terri- fied to render him any a' 1; but fortunately, a lady and gentleman were passing along the river bank at the time, and the gentleman hearing cries, and per- ceiving that a boy was in the water, at once jumped in without divesting himself of any of his clothing. He swam to the boy, but had great difficulty in getting hold of him, but eventually as the boy was going down for the third time he was able to get a firm grasp of him, and swam with him to the side. Hough was carried to some cottages near, where he was stripped of his clothes and artificial rcspiration was employed. Had it not been for the prompt action of the gentleman, who gave no name, nor was he known to the parents of the boy, Hough must inevitably have been drowned. His conduct is most praiseworthy, and he cannot be tc;) highly commended.

HORSE KILLED ON THE RAILWAY.

An accident occurred on the L. & N.-W. Low Level Railway, near to the level crossing at Bank Quay, on Tuesday night. It appears that a gate on the branch line to Messrs. Monks. Hall and Co.'s works was left open, and a valuable three-year- old colt, strayed from Sankey Hall Farm, got through 1, the open gate and thence along the branch line, on to the main line. A luggage train passing at the time knocked down the animal and killed it.

*Unfinished. ODDFELLOWSHIP AT WARRINGTON.

The fortnightly meeting of the meml'ers of the Constitution Lodge of the Independent Order of Oddfellows, Manchester Unity (Warrington Dis- trict), was held at the White Bull Inn Horse- market-st., on Saturday evening, when Inn, more members were initiated,bringing the total numbers of the members of the lodge up to 217. Three of them had been transferred from the Children's Burial Society, of which P.P.O.M. W. Daintith is lodge secretary, having been entered into that society at the respective ages of two days, six days, and one month. This makes a total number trans- ferred from the Children's Burial Society to the Constitution Lodge since 1882 of 31 members, or one-seventh the strength of the lodge. The District Officers were present at the initiation, Prov. G.M. Thomas Rigby acting as lecture master.

FUNERAL OF THE LATE MR. PAYTON.

The funeral took place at the Warrington Ceme- tery on Saturday afternoon, of the late Mr. William Payton, of Bewsey-road, who died on the • previous Wednesday at the age of 48 years. A large number of very beautiful wreaths, &c., had been sent by sympathizing friends. The quarterly session of the East-add-Cheshire District Lodge wee held in the Temperance Hall,on Monday. Bro. H. 11. Drury, D.C.T., presided, sup- ported by Bro. L. Ellison, D.Co., Bro. D. Fog, D.S.J.T., Sister M. E. Armstrong, D.V.T., Bro. B. Battman, D.S., Bro. A. J. Fawkes. D.Treas., Bro. A Dew, D. Chap., Bro. C. F. North, all., Sister A. NI. Anderson, D.Dfiss., Bro. J. The report of the D.S J.T. gave an account of the Work bring done among the juveniles which was satisfactory. The D.Treasurer reported a good balance in hand. The report of the D.Secretary gave figures respect- ing members and progress made throughout East- mid-Cheshire. Resolutions were adopted calling upon the Government to re-introduce the Local Veto Bill next session end to press it through Par- liament; further calling upon Good Templars to select the right candidates for Parish Councils. An adjournment for tea was made after which thp District Lodge re-assembled at 6.30 p.m. A stir- ring address was delivered by Bro. H. H. Drury, D.C.T., followed by Bro. D. Fogg, D.S.J.T. The third degree was conferred on 27 members of the Bonehill Branch Lodge. Votes of thanks were ten- dered to the Latchford friends, after which the session was closed by singing and prayer.

THE PEOPLES COLLEGE.

RESULTS OF EXAMINATIONS.

The Hon. Secretary (Mr. J. J. Crosfield), has re- ceived from the Education Departmept the report of H.M. Inspector, Mr. E.L. Phelps, on his examina- tions of the school in October last when 914 child- ren-518 boys and 396 girls—were present. Of these 131 were in standard 1., 150 in standard 11., 169 in standard 111..155 in standard 1V.,150 in standard V .101 in standard VI., and 58 in standard VII. In addition to the elemnitary and Class Subjects—grammar and gen- Araphy-137 scholars were examined in algebra, 24 in shorthand and 104 girls in domestic economy. As :he result of this examination, the highest possible grants have again been awarded, as has invariably been the ' case in past years, for the elementary work, elass sub- jects, and discipline and organisation. The highest 1 grants have also been made for algebra, shorthand and domestic economy and 65 girls qualified for the cookery grant. The report is as follows :—" Although there is, as hitherto, much to praise in the condition of this school some of the work falls a little short of the highest standard generally attained, notably the arithmetic of the fifth standard and of the girls in the sixth standard; the composition also of the two highest standards shows less originality of thought and facility of expression than usual. Most of the elementary work in the lower standards, on the other hand, is of excellent quality and the class subjects are thoroughly well mastered Both algebra and shorthand have been successfully taught and domestic economy is on the whole good, though a little wanting in intelligence in the first stage." The two infants' schools in connection with the People's College at Bank Quay and Cairo-st. have also received the highest possible grants, and their reports are as follow—Bank Quay Infants' School: "The. premises at Bank Quay have been altered and improved consider- ably. The children are well behaved and talc a lively interest in their work which they do remarkably well.' Cairo-st. Infants' School :" The infants are very wel taught and disciplined." The results of the drawing examination conducted by Captain Royse, It.N., in September last, when 526 boys and 386 girls were examined in standards I. to VII. have been received from the Science and Art. Depart- ment. The mark excellent has again been awarded and 45 cards of merit have been gained by scholars in standards VI. and VII. who passed in each of the three subjects, freehand, geometrical and model drawing. Of ex-Standard VII. scholars, Elizabeth Bayley, Martha J. Bibby. Frederick J. Carlisle, Evelyn Daintith, Annie Granger, Herbert Moss, Eric 0. Leatherbarrow, Charles S. Spensley, and Gertrude Fennelly obtained second class certificates in second grade freehand, and John D. Cannell a first class certificate in the same sub- ject. Elizabeth Bayley, Martha J. Bibby, John P. Hic,rnett, and Gertrude Fennelly obtained second class certificates for second grade model drawing and Evelyn Daintith a first-class certificate for the same subject.

RUNCORN URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL ELECTION. TO THE ELECTORS OF HEATH WARD. LADIES AND GENTLEMEN,— The passing of the Local Government Bill brings us face to face with a new era in municipal government, and the Board of Commissioners now gives place to a District Council. Another matter new to Runcorn is the division of the town into wards. This is a subject that has engaged my earnest attention. I was honoured by the Cheshire County Council to be one of the committee to conduct the inquiry with reference to the suk)ject, and their recommendation was approved by the Council. This, in my opinion, will enable the electors to have more complete control over their repre- sentatives, as they will be able to draw their attention to any matters requiring the consideration of the District Council. My views on the subject of education gene-ally are well known to you. I consider it absolutely necessary that the young people of tee present day should have the full beue fits rising from the grants which the County Council are now giving, and it is immensely satisfactory to find that Runcorn is in the front rank af all urban districts, as they have been recipients of not simply yearly grants for the purposes of higher Cdoteatioe, but a handsome sum towards the new Technical InetitEe, which is approaching completion. My sincere hope Is that fall advantage will be taken of the opportunities provided by the new Institute by these w.shin,; to make progress In the world. My dual position as a Commissioner and County Councillor has enabled me to Ink after the true interests of the town in this matter. You are all aware of the great depression that exists at the present moment in the town, and one of the things that I tkink will facilitate an improvement is the immediate carry- ing out of the agreement of September, 1890, existing between the Commissioners and the •Ship Canal Co. with respect to the provision of a graving doLk. I have hitherto zealously aboured to bring this about, and shall continue my efforts to this end, so that vessels arrivine at Runcorn will not be com- pelled tor) elsewhHe for repairs. This will revive an old industry that gave labour to a large number of carpenters and others. The acquisition of the Water Works by the Commissioners has proved of deeided benefit to the town, for, although numerous difficulties have been encountered during the first year, a substantial profit has resulted. This bears out my expressed anticipations when the scheme was considered bryou. The health of the town is one deserving the closest atten- tion of your representatives, and the increased activity ow the part of the Health Committee during the past two years has met with my warmest support. The reduct on iti the death-rate of late must be gratifying to all of you. All sani- tary improvements shall always receive my earnest atten- tion. There are many other subjects which will call for the attention of your representatives,w hich will include, amongst others, the provision of a recreation ground. This has my entire sympathy, and shall receive, together with all matters affecting the best interests of the town, my most earnest onsideration. It must have fieen-gratttying to you, as a ratepayer, to have found your burdens somewhat lightened by the c ,ntribution f £4OO per annum by the County Couneil for the mainten- ance of the m4in roads of the town. This through my instru- mentality, was secured. I confidently appeal to you for a renewal af your confi- dence which has been uninterrupted for o ver twenty years, and if elected upon the new Urban Distric t Council, you may rely upon my best effort being devoted to Larefully watch all matters concerning the welfare and advancement of our town. I am, Ladies and Gentlemen. Yours faithfully, PHI LIP SPEAKMAN. The Mount, Higher Runcorn, 29 November, 1894.

"We're aw reet neaw !" Eaur good owd teawn's crom full o' zeal, Hoo's put up gates, grand an' genteel ; Wi' Angels watchin' o'er eaur weal— "We're aw reet neaw !" Yes, theer they are, wi' eautstretch'd wings, Wi' nicely ornamented rings An' ovverleokin' men an' things— " We're aw reet neaw !" Sweet omens ov a happier day, Wen nowtiness hez fled away, Ner lads ner wenches goo astray— " V') e're aw reet neaw!' Eaur Anti-Gamblin' folks may step— An' like a bankrupt, shut up shop, Wi' no moor bettin' men to es)p " "We're aw reet neaw !" Thoose " Social Werritins" aw think— • Sitch az bad meyt an' yedd3, drink— Will into cowld oblivion sink— "We're aw reet neaw !" Eaur churches eaur chapels, too, Will hey ev'ry seat an' pew, The faithful legions, good an' true— We're aw reet neaw !". Eaur public-heauses an' hotels, Will change abeaut to springs an' wells, Wheer pious souls con help thersels— " We're aw reet neaw !" Teetotal Resterongs yele meat 1' ev'ry lone an' ev'ry street, An' rowlin' th' money in at neet— " We're aw reet neaw !" Eaur good owd " Chief " will hey less wark To keep his " roberts " up to th' mark— They'l noan be wanted after dark— " We're aw reet neaw !" No lawyers will be needed here— Thay'l waft urn to a far-off sphere, Wheer wig, geawn, fees, aw disappear— " We're aw reet neaw !" " Fareweel to Bridewell, seat o' gloom, Thay'l soon be seaundin' thee thi doom, To make thi in a Mission 12)one !" " We're aw reet neaw !" Then wen aw's settle't, smooth an' nice, We'll aw be singin' wi' one vice, "Thank Angels fer eaur Paradise !" "We're aw reet neaw!" TUM O'PEGS. Roe Bonk, 30, 11, '94. GENERAL NEWS. In the French Chamber of Deputies on Monday, the Madagascar War Credit was carried by a majority of 377 votes to 143. James Willshaw Whitehead was executed in Strange- ways Prison on Tuesday for the murder of his wife at Oldham on the 20th August last. The nomination of candidates for the Briag division, has been fixed for to-day (Saturday), and the polling for Friday, December 7. A shocking murder was perpetrated early on Monday morning in a quiet part of Holland Park-road, Kensing- ton, London, the victim being an unfortunate, named Augusta Dudley. The assassin left behind him a walk- ing stick, but it is feared that it will not afford much of a clue to his identity. The marriage of the Czar of Russia and Princess Alix of Hesse Darmstadt was celebrated at St. Petersburg on Monday amid a scene of enormous popular enthusiasm. Celebration services were held in London, Paris, and elsewhere. The library of Giittingen has a Bible written en palm leaves. There are five thousand three hundred and i seventy-three pages, each made of a single leaf. Accordina to statistics just completed, there are 5,735 steamers flying the British flaz, 810 the German, 510 the I Norwegian, and 503 the French. Mr. Dillon, M.P., speaking ;st Nenaah on Tuesday complained that the Irish were not working the English and Scotch constituencies as they used to work them. They should, he said, place in the hands of the .Irish I Parliamentary party the sinews of war, without which I it was impossible to win.
